Title: The quasi-isometry classes of Galton–Watson trees

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.23882v1

Published Time: Tue, 22 Sep 2026 01:52:51 GMT

Number of Pages: 110

Markdown Content:
THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 

JAYADEV S. ATHREYA AND SASCHA TROSCHEIT 

Dedicated to the memory of Krishna B. Athreya (1939–2023). 

Abstract. We classify, up to quasi-isometry, the large-scale geometry of Galton–Watson trees for every finitely supported offspring distribution. Apart from the trivial finite diameter regimes, we condition on infinite diameter. We find that the remaining classes are the ray class , the 

full tree class (which includes the deterministic binary tree), one chain class (C Λ) for every possible branching semigroup Λ, and the bushy class. Two independent trees almost surely admit a root-preserving quasi-isometry when their offspring distributions belong to the same class and are almost surely not quasi-isometric when they belong to different classes. Any two survival-conditioned supercritical realisations with finitely supported offspring laws nevertheless almost surely admit quasi-isometric embeddings in both directions. We prove that the class of a conditioned infinite realisation depends only on the support of the offspring distribution, not its specific distribution. For offspring distributions supported on {1, 2}, we additionally obtain an explicit exponential tail bound (in the constant D) for the probability of non-existence of a root-preserving D-quasi-isometry. The classification also implies that the corresponding random Cantor boundaries are almost surely quasisymmetrically equivalent. Conditioned on nonextinction, this applies to general strongly separated fractal percolation in the nontrivial supercritical phase, even when the underlying self-similar iterated function systems and retention parameters differ. We also classify two families of binary trees with continuous random branching times. Our proofs use new automorphism-matching theorems for random graph labellings of trees, based on contraction estimates for mismatch potentials. These matching results for Markov labellings on the binary tree are fairly general and may be of independent interest. The quasi-isometry classification, mutual embeddability and both matching theorems are formally verified in Lean.

Part 1. Introduction and main results 

1. Introduction 

Random branching. Galton–Watson trees are among the canonical models of random branch-ing. They arise throughout probability, from population dynamics and percolation to random graphs and random fractal constructions, and often serve as local models for more complicated random objects. Their theory of extinction, generation growth, and limiting population size is classical. We refer to Harris [28] and Athreya–Ney [7] for the foundations of the subject, and to Lyons–Peres [36] for a modern account of branching processes and probability on trees. 

Large-scale geometry. By contrast, much less is known about the large-scale metric geometry of an entire realisation. With the graph metric, a Galton–Watson tree is a random metric space, and its geometry can be studied up to quasi-isometry , the equivalence relation that retains distances at large scales while discarding uniformly bounded errors. Quasi-isometry is central to geometric group theory, where it organises spaces, in particular Cayley graphs of finitely generated groups, by their coarse geometry, a perspective initiated by, among others, Gromov. See de la Harpe [21] and Bridson–Haefliger [16] for more background. 

Quasi-isometries and bi-Lipschitz equivalence. The deterministic theory of branching trees (and their interpretation, for regular trees, as Cayley graphs of free groups) suggests a considerable range of behaviours. All regular trees of degree at least three are bi-Lipschitz equivalent by Papasoglu [46], and all bushy trees of bounded valence belong to one quasi-isometry class by Mosher–Sageev–Whyte [43, §2.1]. Whyte [52] further showed that, for non-amenable uniformly discrete spaces of bounded geometry, every quasi-isometry lies a bounded distance from a bi-Lipschitz map. Lindquist [33] obtained a related result for spaces satisfying linear isoperimetric inequalities and applied it to regularly branching trees and hyperbolic fillings of compact Ahlfors-regular spaces. These results cover the homogeneous case in which branching occurs at a uniformly bounded distance from every vertex. The distinction between quasi-isometry and bi-Lipschitz equivalence remains important in familiar coarse models. For n ⩾ 2, every separated net in Rn is quasi-isometric to Zn, but Burago–Kleiner [17] and McMullen [38] constructed nets that are not bi-Lipschitz equivalent to Zn. Their obstruction comes from density fluctuations across scales, a phenomenon excluded in Whyte’s non-amenable setting. For more recent results in this direction (focusing on Delone sets linked to substitution tilings), see Smilansky–Solomon [50]. 

Nonhomogeneity. Galton–Watson trees need not be homogeneous in the sense of large-scale geometry. For example, if the probability of having a single offspring is positive, infinite trees almost surely produce arbitrarily long chains without branching, while if the probability of having zero offspring is positive, leaves will produce finite bushes of arbitrarily large depth. Neither feature is confined to a bounded part of the tree, and each changes its large-scale geometry. The classical theory therefore does not decide whether two independent realisations from the same offspring law are quasi-isometric, still less whether realisations from different laws can have the same coarse geometry. THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 3

Random metric spaces. This is an instance of a wider problem for random metric spaces: when do two independent realisations have the same large-scale geometry almost surely? Benjamini [10] discusses this question in several settings. For random subsets of the line, Peled [47] obtained quantitative finite-scale estimates, and Basu–Sly [9] proved that two independent Poisson processes are almost surely roughly isometric. Related embedding questions for independent, identically distributed (i.i.d.) fields were studied by Basu–Sidoravicius–Sly [8]. By contrast, Li– Yu–Zheng [34] recently proved that two independent random subsets of a product of regular trees are almost surely not quasi-isometric. Galton–Watson trees provide a natural rank-one family: their branching favours flexibility, but their chains and finite bushes create inhomogeneities of flatness on every scale. 

Quasisymmetry. There is also a boundary version of the same geometric problem. A locally finite tree is hyperbolic, and a quasi-isometry induces a quasisymmetric map between its visual boundaries. This is part of the general correspondence between the coarse geometry of hyperbolic spaces and the quasisymmetric geometry of their boundaries developed by Bonk–Schramm [15] and described by Buyalo–Schroeder [18], inspired by classical results of Mostow. For a non-ray Galton–Watson tree conditioned on having infinite diameter, the boundary is a random Cantor space. In standard geometric constructions such boundaries are coding spaces of random fractals. Thus a classification of the trees produces maps between random fractals, rather than only an equality between numerical invariants, something we will further discuss below. 

Classification. In this paper we give a complete quasi-isometry classification for every finitely supported offspring distribution. The answer is controlled by the occurrence of leaves and unary vertices, together with one arithmetic invariant generated by the possible branching excesses. In particular, only the support of the offspring distribution is of relevance for the classification, not the actual probabilities after the appropriate conditioning is satisfied. 1.1. The classification. Subcritical Galton–Watson trees and trees with a non-deterministic critical offspring law are finite almost surely. The same is true of every extinct realisation of a supercritical law. Since all finite metric spaces are quasi-isometric, these realisations form the single class (Fin) and it remains to classify infinite realisations. The only nonstandard invariant in the answer is the branching semigroup :

Definition 1.1 (Branching semigroup) . Suppose that an offspring distribution θ satisfies θ0 = 0. Its branching semigroup is the additive submonoid Λθ := ⟨k − 1 : k ⩾ 1 and θk > 0⟩ ⊆ N0.

Branching, clustering, and semigroups. A vertex with k children contributes the excess 

k − 1. Thus Λ θ records the possible total excesses that branch points can realise. If θ2 > 0, then Λ θ = N0. Conversely, every additive submonoid Λ of N0 generated by a nonempty finite set of positive integers is the branching semigroup of some finitely supported offspring law with 

θ0 = 0 < θ 1 < 1. We call these the possible branching semigroups . The branching semigroup Λ θ

is a coarse invariant only when long unary chains can isolate such clusters (that is, when θ0 = 0, 

θ1 > 0). For each such Λ, we write (C Λ) for the class of distributions with branching semigroup Λ. The following theorem gives the complete root-preserving quasi-isometry classification. 

Theorem 1.2 (Complete quasi-isometry classification) . All finite Galton–Watson realisations form the quasi-isometry class (Fin) , and no finite tree is quasi-isometric to an infinite tree. Let θ and θ′ be finitely supported offspring distributions, each either supercritical or with 

θ1 = 1 , and let T , T ′ be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Every such distribution belongs to exactly one of the following classes: (R) θ1 = 1 , the ray class, in which T is the ray. (F) θ0 = θ1 = 0 , the full tree class, in which T admits a quasi-isometry to the binary tree. 

(C Λ) θ0 = 0 < θ 1 < 1 and Λθ = Λ , the chain class indexed by the branching semigroups Λ.(B) θ0 > 0, the bushy class. Two independent realisations belonging to the same class almost surely admit a root-preserving quasi-isometry. If they belong to different classes, then they are almost surely not quasi-isometric. 4 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Regimes. We retain (R), (F), (C), and (B) for the four coarse regimes used to organise the proof, with regime (C) denoting the union of the classes (C Λ). See Figure 1 for illustrative representatives of these classes. 

Independent realisations. The classes in Theorem 1.2 completely resolve the classification problem for independent realisations of every finitely supported offspring law. In particular, two independent realisations from the same law, conditioned on having infinite diameter when necessary, almost surely admit a root-preserving quasi-isometry. The cross-law statement is more rigid due to the class structure. Full trees have the deterministic class of the binary tree, while the bushy regime has one universal class despite the presence of arbitrarily deep finite dead ends. The chain regime splits into the classes (C Λ), since long unary stretches isolate finite clusters of branch points and make their total excesses visible at large scales. This is precisely the information retained by Λ θ.

GCDs and tails. A finitely generated additive submonoid of N0 with a positive generator contains every sufficiently large multiple of the greatest common divisor of its positive generators. Thus the greatest common divisor determines the eventual tail, but finitely many exceptional gaps can still distinguish two branching semigroups. See Niven–Zuckerman–Montgomery [44, Section 1.2]. 

Embeddability. If we drop the coarse-surjectivity requirement and ask only for a quasi-isometric embedding, the distinctions between the supercritical classes disappear. In particular, the branching semigroup no longer obstructs comparison in either direction. We write B for the rooted binary tree. 

Theorem 1.3 (Mutual embeddability) . Let θ be a finitely supported supercritical offspring distribution, and let T be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Almost surely, there are quasi-isometric embeddings 

T ↪→ B and B ↪→ T .

Pruning. We prove Theorem 1.3 in §23, using the classification to choose a convenient repre-sentative in each chain class and pruning an infinite regular subtree from that representative. 

A strict order. By composition, any two independent survival-conditioned supercritical realisa-tions with finitely supported offspring laws almost surely admit quasi-isometric embeddings in both directions, even when their laws belong to different classes in Theorem 1.2. Together with the elementary finite and ray comparisons, this gives the strict order Fin ↪→ Ray ↪→ Supercritical .

Here each arrow denotes quasi-isometric embeddability, with no embedding in the reverse direction. The finite and ray categories each consist of one quasi-isometry class. All full tree, chain and bushy classes belong to the last category, but remain distinct under quasi-isometry. In particular, mutual quasi-isometric embeddability does not imply quasi-isometry .

Random branching-time. While we only deal with finitely supported offspring distributions in our main results, the random branching-time model in §7 reaches beyond this setting. Its unit-time coarse graining can have unbounded arity while retaining the quasi-isometry type of a weighted binary tree. Thus Theorem 7.2 identifies classes represented by some unbounded-arity random trees. We do not attempt a classification for arbitrary offspring laws with infinite support. 1.2. Quantitative and geometric consequences. Universality and effectiveness. The classification is qualitative, but the basic chain model admits an explicit estimate for the quasi-isometry constant. It is also the simplest setting in which the matching mechanism of this paper appears.

θ2 = 1 , and let T (ω), T (ω′) be two independent Galton–Watson trees with offspring distribution 

θ. Then, almost surely, there is a root-preserving quasi-isometry T (ω) → T (ω′). Moreover, if

The top row shows regime (F), with support {2, 3, 4}, and regime (B), with support {0, 1, 2, 3}. That is, the failure probability has an exponentially small upper bound in the quasi-isometry constant D. It may be interesting to see if this upper bound is sharp (that is, if there is a corresponding lower bound for the failure probability). The exponential estimate makes a zero–one law unnecessary, although Remark 13.2 records one that applies to these realisations. Analogous, less explicit estimates drive the cross-law and bushy-regime arguments in Parts 2 and 3. 

From QI to QS. Quasi-isometries between proper hyperbolic spaces induce quasisymmetries of their visual boundaries. Our classification therefore has the following random-fractal consequence. 

Corollary 1.5 (Quasisymmetry of the boundaries) . Let θ and θ′ satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem 1.2, suppose that neither belongs to the ray class, and suppose that they belong to the same class. Let T (ω) and T ′(ω′) be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Almost surely, their visual boundaries are compact, perfect, totally disconnected metric spaces, and there is a quasisymmetric homeomorphism 

(∂T (ω), d vis ) −→ (∂T ′(ω′), d vis ).

Supercritical fractal percolation. In particular, consider two independent strongly separated fractal percolation constructions in the nontrivial supercritical phase. Their underlying iterated function systems (IFSs) and retention probabilities may both differ. Conditioned on nonextinction, their limit sets are almost surely quasisymmetrically equivalent. The realisations in Figure 2 illustrate this conclusion when the IFS is fixed and only the retention probability changes. We prove the more general consequence in Corollary 3.1. 

Quasisymmetry and conformal dimension. Quasisymmetry is a central equivalence relation in analysis on metric spaces and in the geometry of self-similar sets. See David–Semmes [20] and Heinonen [29] for more background. Conformal dimension in the sense of Pansu [45] is the infimum of the Hausdorff dimensions in a quasisymmetric gauge, see Mackay–Tyson [37] for a systematic account. Bonk–Meyer [14] characterised the metric trees quasisymmetrically equivalent to the continuum self-similar tree as precisely the trivalent, uniformly branching quasiconformal trees. Chrontsios-Garitsis–Ioannidis–Vellis [19, Theorem 1.9] extended this classification to every finite valence: for each n ⩾ 3, the uniformly n-branching quasiconformal trees form a single quasisymmetry class represented by a self-similar tree. Leaves and unary chains are exactly what break the corresponding uniformity in our setting. Beyond trees, Bonk’s uniformisation theorem [12] maps suitable Sierpi´ nski carpets to round carpets, whereas Bonk–Merenkov [13] proved that standard square carpets are quasisymmetrically rigid. 

Mandelbrot percolation. For random fractal geometry, the classical grid-based model is 

Mandelbrot percolation . Conditioned on nonextinction, Rossi–Suomala [48] showed that almost every classical fractal percolation realisation has conformal dimension strictly below its Hausdorff dimension. For the fat variant, Anttila–Eriksson-Bique–Py¨ or¨ al¨ a [4] showed that, under the same conditioning, almost every realisation is minimal for conformal dimension. For the dense variant they obtained the analogous conclusion for power quasisymmetries. Fraser–Tyson [25] studied the distortion of intermediate dimensions under quasiconformal and quasisymmetric maps, with consequences for Mandelbrot percolation realisations. We refer the reader to the recent survey by Kolossv´ ary–Troscheit [30] for more background. We highlight here that these results compute or bound quasisymmetric invariants, whereas our conclusion instead produces the stronger fact of quasisymmetric equivalence between two independent realisations in strongly separated models. 

Random branching times. Our matching theorems Theorems 5.1 and 6.1 also apply when the binary genealogy is deterministic and the times between successive branch points are random. In §7, we introduce a family which permits branch points to be arbitrarily close and arbitrarily far apart. We prove that two independent realisations, even with different parameters, are almost surely quasi-isometric. This family has non-doubling boundary and is separated from the shifted-exponential model, whose branching times are bounded below and whose boundary is doubling. Finite truncations of both families are shown in Figure 4. 

Brownian trees and spheres. Finally, we highlight another connection of branching processes with mathematical physics. Critical branching leads to continuum random geometry. The Brownian continuum random tree of Aldous [1, 2] is the universal scaling limit of critical THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 7

Galton–Watson trees with finite offspring variance conditioned to have n vertices, as n → ∞ . A related object from random geometry is the Brownian sphere. Le Gall [32] and Miermont [39] established convergence to it from uniform quadrangulations and related families of planar maps. While it is known, see Troscheit [51], that neither the Brownian tree nor the Brownian sphere admits a quasisymmetric embedding into a doubling metric space, a finer question asks whether two independent realisations are quasisymmetrically equivalent almost surely. This was recently answered in the negative for the Brownian sphere by Miller–Tian [42], who also computed the conformal dimensions of the continuum random tree and Brownian sphere as 1 and 2, respectively. See [40, 41] for details. The natural question that is left open between our regime of supercritical branching processes and that of the Brownian sphere is that for the continuum random tree, which was also asked by Bonk–Meyer in [14, Problem 9.1]. A finer analysis of our matching results may be able to answer this question. 1.3. Ideas of the proof. The main new tool we introduce in this paper is an automorphism-matching principle for random graph-indexed labellings of trees, which are of independent interest. We first contract or decompose a Galton–Watson realisation until its coarse geometry is recorded by a random choice of graph-indexed labels on a branching skeleton . A tree automorphism of the skeleton then matches two independent label fields while allowing bounded local errors, and local quasi-isometries between compatible labels glue to a global quasi-isometry. 

Graph labellings. In many of our settings, the skeleton will be an infinite binary tree, and the labels at each vertex will be independent and take values in an arbitrary countable graph. The graph records which local pieces are compatible, and a single scalar potential controls the matching failures. This gives Theorem 5.1. More generally, the skeleton has several possible arities. To compare such skeletons on the binary tree, we replace a split with k children by a finite binary tree with k leaves. Several successive binary splits may now represent a single original branch point, so the resulting labels are dependent. We describe this dependence by a tree-indexed Markov field, in the sense of Benjamini–Peres [11], and extend the matching argument to this setting in Theorem 6.1. The binary replacements must also allow us to compare trees with different branching laws. In the chain regime, equality of the branching semigroups lets us choose compatible replacements. In the bushy regime, the finite decorations provide enough flexibility to compare any two offspring laws. Both matching theorems are independent of Galton–Watson trees and may be useful in other tree-indexed matching problems. 

Embeddings. For Theorem 1.3, the classification itself becomes a tool. Within each chain class, we choose an offspring law concentrated at a large arity whose excess belongs to the given branching semigroup. We repeatedly discard vertices that do not have this arity or have too few children remaining from the previous round. A binomial estimate gives positive probability that the root survives every round, and independent trials along a ray locate a surviving vertex at finite depth almost surely. The retained descendants contain an isometric binary tree, whose embedding transfers to the original law by quasi-isometry. For a bushy realisation, we apply this argument to its survival skeleton and leave the finite bushes unused. The reverse embedding follows from the bounded offspring support. 1.4. Lean formalisation. Overview. The Galton–Watson classification, mutual embeddability and the i.i.d. and Markov matching theorems have been formalised in Lean 4 over the mathematical library Mathlib. The Lean files are available at https://github.com/sascha2718/qi-trees-and-matching-lean 

and will also be submitted to Palomar . The development consists of four libraries. The first, 

BranchingProcess , constructs Galton–Watson trees from i.i.d. offspring fields and proves the required measurability, extinction, conditioning, Harris decomposition, progeny tails, pruning estimates and coarse geometric results. GraphMatching proves the finite and infinite i.i.d. automorphism-matching theorems, for both leaf and full labellings. GraphMarkovMatching 

proves the general Markov matching theorem at finite and infinite height, together with its quantitative bounds. ChainClasses formalises the chain and shape encodings, their laws, the coupling and geometric transfer arguments, and the universality, separation and classification of 8 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

conditioned infinite realisations, both within and across arbitrary bounded-support offspring laws. In particular, all four infinite classes (R), (F), (C Λ) and (B), as well as the qualitative and quantitative two-value results, are covered. The development also proves Theorem 1.3, mutual embeddability for two survival-conditioned laws and the strict hierarchy with finite trees and the ray. The pruning estimates, conditional law of the retained descendant tree and regular-subtree construction are proved in BranchingProcess . The library ChainClasses locates a retained vertex almost surely, proves the explicit first-child-ray tail bound, and transfers the embedding through the classification. 

Challenge files. A single challenge file, Challenge.lean , imports Mathlib and nothing from these four libraries, and defines the vocabulary needed to state thirteen headline results of this paper: the four finite- and infinite-height conclusions of Theorem 6.1; the leaf and full finite-height bounds Equations (5.2) and (5.3) and the infinite-tree conclusion of Theorem 5.1(2); the complete classification of Theorem 1.2 in three statements, that bounded connected graphs are quasi-isometric to a point, that a law with mean at most one and θ1̸ = 1 dies out almost surely, and the classification itself over the unconditioned laws, finite class included; the mutual-embeddability statement of Theorem 1.3; and the qualitative conclusion and quantitative bound of Theorem 1.4, the latter being (1.1). Its theorems have the placeholder proof sorry by design. Following the challenge–solution methodology of the comparator audit tool ( https: //github.com/leanprover/comparator ), the proofs live in the separate file Solution.lean ,and the audit checks that they prove exactly the challenge statements. The checked libraries and solution are free of sorry and assume no external mathematical result as an axiom. Only the standard axioms of propositional extensionality, choice, and quotient soundness are assumed. 

Lacunae. The formalisation does not include the random branching-time theorem of §7 or the external passage from quasi-isometries to quasisymmetries of visual boundaries used in Corollary 1.5 and its fractal applications. Formalising them would require work on an inter-face between the different categories rather than checking their otherwise simple proofs. For a declaration-by-declaration correspondence and the precise statement-level boundaries, see Appendix B. 1.5. Organisation. In the remainder of Part 1 we fix the common notation, prove the boundary applications, outline the proof of the classification, and state the i.i.d. and Markov matching theorems. We then apply the i.i.d. theorem to random branching times in §7 and end with the further directions in §8. Part 2 develops the binary-support model, including the explicit estimate in Theorem 1.4 and the treatment of finite bushes. Part 3 restates the Markov matching theorem in full detail, proves it, and assembles the full classification. It concludes with the embedding theorem and strict hierarchy in §23. A more detailed breakdown of the proof ideas can be found in §4 after we introduce more notation. We strongly urge the reader to read the remainder of Part 1 before reading Parts 2 and 3. This gives a more concise introduction to our proofs. We further recommend studying Part 2 before Part 3. While the results in Part 3 strictly subsume those in Part 2, extending the techniques to offspring distributions with arity greater than 2 requires more technical work. A glossary and symbol list can be found in Appendix A, which collects the notation used throughout the paper. It is followed by the Lean appendix in Appendix B, which lists the declaration correspondences.

They accept full responsibility for their contents. 2. Notation and preliminaries 

We collect the conventions used throughout the paper. We first describe rooted trees and their Galton–Watson laws, and then recall the two metric equivalence notions used for the trees and their boundaries. 2.1. Words and rooted trees. For N ∈ N, let N = N (N ) be the tree of finite words over 

{1, . . . , N }. Its root ∅ is the empty word, and v is joined to vj for every letter j. We equip N

with the graph metric 

d(v, w ) = |v| + |w| − 2|v ∧ w|,

where v ∧ w is the longest common prefix. The binary tree is B = N (2). We write BY (y, r ) for the closed ball in a metric space Y and Aut( B) for the rooted automorphism group of B.2.2. Galton–Watson trees. Let θ = ( θ0, . . . , θ N ) be a finitely supported probability distribu-tion on N0, and let ( χv)v∈N be independent random variables with this law. The associated 10 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Galton–Watson tree T ⊆ N contains the root, and whenever v ∈ T , it contains exactly the children v1, . . . , vχ v. We always use the induced graph metric. The law is supercritical when 

> N∑
> k=0

kθ k > 1.

Unless stated otherwise, realisations in the infinite classification are conditioned on having infinite diameter. Distinct realisations appearing in the same statement are built from independent coordinate families. We use P and E locally for the probability and expectation under the law specified in the current statement or section, including its conditioning on infinite diameter and any independent auxiliary variables. We write P∗ when we need to distinguish the survival-conditioned law explicitly from the original Galton–Watson law. 2.3. Quasi-isometries. Definition 2.1 (Quasi-isometry) . Let ( Y1, d 1) and ( Y2, d 2) be metric spaces. We say that a map ψ : ( Y1, d 1) → (Y2, d 2) is an ( A, B, C )-quasi-isometry if (1) A−1d1(x, y ) − B ⩽ d2(ψ(x), ψ (y)) ⩽ Ad 1(x, y ) + B for all x, y ∈ Y1;(2) every y ∈ Y2 lies at distance at most C from ψ(Y1), where A ⩾ 1 and B, C ⩾ 0. For rooted spaces, a quasi-isometry is root-preserving if it sends the source root to the target root. Changing only the image of the root is a bounded change of the map and therefore preserves the quasi-isometry property, although its constants may change. If a map satisfies only the metric inequality in Definition 2.11, we call it a quasi-isometric embedding . We write X ≼ Y when such an embedding X ↪ → Y exists, and X ≺ Y when X ≼ Y

but Y̸ ≼ X. Quasi-isometric embeddings compose, so ≼ is transitive. Note that a quasi-isometry from Y1 to Y2 induces a quasi-isometry from Y2 to Y1 and hence being quasi-isometric is an equivalence relation. We write Y1 ≃ Y2 when a quasi-isometry exists. Throughout the paper, a 

D-quasi-isometry means a ( D, D, D )-quasi-isometry. We do not aim to optimise constants. 2.4. Visual boundaries and quasisymmetries. For an infinite locally finite rooted tree T ,let ∂T be the set of infinite rays from the root. For ξ, ζ ∈ ∂T , set 

dvis (ξ, ζ ) = 

> {

2−| ξ∧ζ|, ξ̸ = ζ, 

0, ξ = ζ. (2.1) This is a compact ultrametric boundary. 

Definition 2.2 (Quasisymmetry) . Let η : [0 , ∞) → [0 , ∞) be a homeomorphism. A home-omorphism ψ : ( Y1, d 1) → (Y2, d 2) is η-quasisymmetric if, for all distinct x, y, z ∈ Y1 and all 

t > 0, 

d1(x, z ) ⩽ td 1(y, z ) =⇒ d2(ψ(x), ψ (z)) ⩽ η(t)d2(ψ(y), ψ (z)) .

We refer to Buyalo–Schroeder [18] for visual boundaries and to Heinonen [29] for quasisym-metric maps. 3. Boundaries and quasisymmetry 

Boundaries. We now prove the boundary consequence stated in Corollary 1.5. Recall that the ray regime is excluded there because its boundary is a singleton rather than a Cantor space. 

Proof of Corollary 1.5. By Theorem 1.2, the two trees are almost surely quasi-isometric. The unit-edge geometric realisation of each tree is a proper geodesic 0-hyperbolic space, and the inclusion of the vertex set is a quasi-isometry. The two geometric realisations are therefore quasi-isometric. Buyalo–Schroeder [18, Theorem 5.2.17] gives a quasisymmetric homeomorphism between their Gromov boundaries for any choices of visual metrics. The end boundary is canonically the same as that of the vertex tree, and the metric in (2.1) is the visual metric based at the root with parameter log 2. THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 11 

Figure 2. Finite approximations to two fractal percolation realisations for the IFS formed by the four corner similarities of ratio 0 .49. The retention probabilities are 0 .99 on the left and 0 .7 on the right. The end boundary of an infinite locally finite rooted tree is compact and totally disconnected. Outside the ray and finite regimes, almost surely every vertex of the survival skeleton has a descendant with at least two infinite child subtrees. This is immediate in the full tree regime. In the chain and bushy regimes, the branching property gives probability zero to a surviving subtree whose skeleton remains a single ray forever. Applying this fact at the countably many vertices shows that no boundary point is isolated. Both boundaries are therefore perfect. □

3.1. Random Cantor boundaries. The visual boundary records the successive branching choices along an infinite ray. Unary chains produce gaps between branching scales, while finite bushes disappear from the boundary but affect the geometry used to construct the quasi-isometry. Thus Corollary 1.5 shows that two independent realisations have the same quasisymmetric type rather than merely showing that a numerical invariant such as Hausdorff or conformal dimension agrees. 

Separated systems. The conclusion persists when the two constructions use different strongly separated systems. The point is that the retained trees are all in regime (B), while the finitely many contraction ratios only replace the unit edges by uniformly comparable lengths. The two same-IFS examples in Figure 2 are therefore a special case of the following corollary. We use 

fractal percolation for this general IFS construction. Mandelbrot percolation is the special case in which the IFS is generated by subdividing an n-dimensional cube into congruent smaller 

n-dimensional cubes. 

Corollary 3.1 (Fractal percolation across different IFSs) . Let Φ, Φ′ be finite strongly separated iterated function systems consisting of M, M ′ ⩾ 2 contracting similarities, and retain each child independently with probability p, p ′, respectively. Suppose that 

0 < p, p ′ < 1 and pM, p ′M ′ > 1.

Let E and E′ be independent fractal percolation limit sets for these constructions. Conditioned on both sets being nonempty, they are almost surely quasisymmetrically equivalent. Proof of Corollary 3.1. The retained words for Φ and Φ ′ form Galton–Watson trees T and T ′

with offspring laws Bin( M, p ) and Bin( M ′, p ′), respectively. These laws are supercritical and have respective supports {0, . . . , M } and {0, . . . , M ′}, so both belong to regime (B). By Theorem 1.2, conditioned on nonextinction, the unit-edge trees T and T ′ are almost surely quasi-isometric. Write ri, 1 ⩽ i ⩽ M , for the contraction ratios (so ri ∈ (0 , 1)) of the maps in Φ, and write r′

> i

,1 ⩽ i ⩽ M ′, for those of the maps in Φ ′. Give an edge of T ending in the letter i length − log ri,and define the edge lengths of T ′ analogously using r′

> i

. Since both systems are finite, the identity 12 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

map from each unit-edge tree to its weighted version is bi-Lipschitz. The two weighted trees are therefore quasi-isometric, and the boundary theorem used in the proof of Corollary 1.5 gives a quasisymmetric homeomorphism between their visual boundaries. It remains to identify these boundaries with the random limit sets. If two coding rays have longest common prefix u, their visual distance in the weighted tree is the product of the contraction ratios along u. Strong separation bounds the Euclidean distance of their images above and below by fixed multiples of the same product. The coding maps from ∂T and ∂T ′ to 

E and E′, respectively, are consequently bi-Lipschitz. Composing these coding maps with the boundary homeomorphism proves the claim. □

Percolation structures. We note that choosing the same percolation structure was purely for convenience and all we required here was that their associated Galton–Watson trees lie in the same classification regime of Theorem 1.2. Analogous results hold for much wider classes of stochastically self-similar fractals. Further, the limit sets of those constructions are totally disconnected and have conformal dimension 0. Our result goes beyond that by saying that these different realisations of fractal percolation in the same regime are also quasisymmetrically equivalent. This is possibly counter-intuitive as these sets are not uniformly perfect (they have lower dimension 0), yet are maximal with respect to the Assouad dimension [24, 30]. 4. Proof strategy 

Scheme. The regimes illustrated in Figure 1 require different local approaches, but each case of quasi-isometric equivalence is proved by the same strategy: realisation −→ branching skeleton with local pieces −→ quantised random labels 

−→ automorphism matching −→ (root preserving) quasi-isometry .

Labellings and matchings. The construction of the labels and the matching theorem change from one regime to another, but the transfer back to the original realisations always glues uniformly controlled local maps. 4.1. The two-value chain model. When 0 < θ 1 < 1, θ1 + θ2 = 1, contracting maximal unary chains leaves the binary tree B. The contracted lengths form an i.i.d. field of geometrically distributed random variables on B. To try and build a quasi-isometry depending on a parameter 

D > 1, we quantise a length λ by its logarithmic (base D) scale, that is, ⌊log D λ⌋. Adjacent quantised labels correspond to lengths comparable within a factor of order D2, and Theorem 5.1 aligns all labels by one automorphism. The transfer theorem then produces a ( D2 + 3)-quasi-isometry. The potential estimate (13.1) gives (1.1), and taking the union over D proves almost-sure equivalence. 4.2. Leaves and finite shapes. When θ0 > 0, a realisation conditioned on having infinite diameter splits into an infinite skeleton and finite bushes attached to it, the Harris decomposition 

(see Lyons [35]). This decomposition makes the decorations independent after conditioning on the skeleton. A local label now records a finite decorated shape rather than one chain length. We replace the shape space by a countable net at scale D, use entropy dilution to bound its graph potential, apply the matching theorem, and glue the matched local quasi-isometries. Part 2 carries this out for offspring supported on {0, 1, 2}.4.3. General arity and two offspring laws. At general bounded support, the skeleton has several possible arities. We replace each split with k children by a finite binary tree with k leaves. Its root carries the label of the original piece, and its other internal vertices carry the label of a one-vertex piece. These replacements have bounded height for each fixed pair of offspring laws, so the resulting assemblies are quasi-isometric to the original trees. The choice of a replacement determines where the next independent labels occur. We record the position within each replacement as a type, obtaining a tree-indexed Markov field to which Theorem 6.1 applies. To compare two branching laws, we choose the replacements from a common family of smaller binary trees. In the bushy regime, the reduced skeletons both allow THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 13 

binary splits. In the chain regime, a common family exists whenever the branching semigroups agree. We can then match the labels and glue the local comparisons as in the binary case. 4.4. Coarse obstructions. The negative results use deterministic features that occur at arbitrarily large scales, namely: 

• Three diverging rays rule out the ray regime. 

• Long unary chains create thin balls and separate the chain regime from the full tree regime. 

• Arbitrarily deep finite dead ends separate the bushy regime from the leafless regimes. Finally, if m ∈ Λθ \ Λθ′ , the first chain law almost surely produces isolated clusters with total excess m and arbitrarily long outgoing arms. No realisation from the second law can reproduce the corresponding coarse star. These arguments are proved alongside the classification results in Parts 2 and 3. 5. Matching random labellings 

Automorphism matching. The common probabilistic step is the following automorphism-matching theorem. Let G = ( V, E ) be a countable graph and let μ be a probability measure on 

V . We view the vertex set V as a set of possible labels , and will call maps from fixed binary trees to G labellings . We say labels v, w ∈ V are compatible when dG(v, w ) ⩽ 1. Write

Leaf and full labellings. Let Bh be the complete binary tree of height h. A G-leaf labelling 

is a map x : {1, 2}h → V assigning a label to each leaf. A full G-labelling is a map x : Bh → V

assigning a label to every vertex. An automorphism matches two labellings, either leaf or full, if it sends every relevant vertex to one carrying a compatible label.

Decay and finite-height problems. The decay in (5.2) solves the finite-height leaf problem. For full labellings, K˝ onig’s infinity lemma [31] turns compatible finite-height automorphisms into one automorphism of the infinite tree, so the uniform estimate (5.3) gives the stated infinite conclusion. Markov labellings. The independent-label theorem does not directly apply when a split with several children is represented by successive binary splits. As described in §4, the original vertices retain their random labels and the inserted vertices receive the label of a one-vertex piece. The branching choices determine where new random labels occur, creating dependence between the labels. We describe this dependence by assigning a type to each vertex. The next theorem applies to general transitions between types, including the binary encodings used in the classification. 6.1. The process and its one-site potential. We use the graph G = ( V, E ), label law μ,compatible mass b(v) and potential ηG,α (μ) of §5. Fix a distinguished label 0 ∈ V . In addition to comparing two independent labels with law μ, we must compare such a label with 0. The latter comparison fails with probability 1 − b(0). For α ⩾ 1, we therefore set 

ζG,α (μ) := max 

> {

ηG,α (μ), 1 − b(0) 

b(0) α

> }

. (6.1) For fixed G, we abbreviate the two potentials by ηα(μ) and ζα(μ), omitting μ when it is fixed as well. We make the convention that ζα = ∞ when b(0) = 0. The second term in (6.1) can be necessary even when μ(0) = 0. For example, take G to be the path 0–1–2 and let μ = (1 − t)δ2 + tδ 1, where 0 < t < 1. The supported labels are mutually compatible, so ηα = 0, whereas b(0) = t. A root with prescribed label 0 is incompatible with an independent root with law μ with probability 1 − t, and the corresponding term is (1 − t)/t α. Thus ηα alone cannot control comparisons involving inserted vertices, whose label is 0. If μ(0) ⩾ p > 0, the summand of ηα at 0 gives 

ζα ⩽ ηα/p. 

Sets of types. Let I be a nonempty countable set of types and choose a subset Iμ ⊆ I . At a vertex whose type belongs to Iμ, we draw a label with law μ; at every other vertex we put the label 0. For each t ∈ I , let Pt be a probability law on I × I . At a vertex of type t, independently of its label, we draw the types of its two children with law Pt. Conditioned on these types, the two descendant processes are independent and follow the same rule. We use independent draws at distinct vertices. 

Matching. Matching has the same meaning as in §5: an automorphism of the rooted binary tree must pair compatible labels at every vertex. We emphasise here: It need not preserve types. 

For independent processes started at s, t ∈ I , write Mh(s, t ) for the event of a match through height h, and M∞(s, t ) for a match of the infinite labellings. Two different models are included by taking the disjoint union of their type sets and letting each transition law stay within its own model. 6.2. The finite-type hypotheses. We now state the additional assumptions for a finite type set. Partition I into classes I0, . . . , Ig−1, where g ⩾ 1. All types receiving random labels belong to I0, and a type in Ij has both child types in Ij+1 , with indices read modulo g. Note that our model allows random types for children with deterministic labels. Thus we require 

Iμ ⊆ I 0, supp Pt ⊆ I j+1 × I j+1 (t ∈ I j ).

We allow g = 1, when there is a single class I0 = I. Figure 3 illustrates this case and a model with two classes. A possible type path follows either child in transitions of positive probability. Constants and type transitions. We give a more detailed restatement in §16, specifying the uniform dependence of the constants on the type transitions and the cost of using ηα. When every label in the support of μ is compatible with 0, we have b(0) = 1. The theorem then imposes no restriction on the type transitions. It allows arbitrarily many vertices labelled 0 between successive draws from μ, or branches with no further draws. Otherwise, the finite-type assumptions ensure that comparisons between random and prescribed labels can be controlled uniformly in the height. For two finitely supported arity laws with the same branching semigroup, we construct finite binary replacements satisfying the theorem’s hypotheses. This supplies the THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 17 

matching needed for the general classification. We prove the theorem in §16 and construct the replacements in §20. 7. Random branching times 

Age-dependent branching. The chain encoding separates the binary genealogy from the distances between successive branch points. We now use this observation when the genealogy is the deterministic binary tree but its edges have independent random lengths. Equivalently, every particle waits for a random time and then splits into two particles. This is an age-dependent branching model rather than a Galton–Watson model, but its metric geometry is governed by the same matching theorem. Given a function l : B → (0 , ∞), let Xl be the geometric realisation of B in which both edges from w to its children have length l(w). Thus Xl is a metric tree whose branch vertices are indexed by B.

Branching-time densities. We consider two families of branching-time densities. For λ > 0, put

Collapsing. The branching-time interpretation also gives a discrete model with unbounded arity. Collapse each connected component of 

{x ∈ Xl : n ⩽ dXl (∅, x ) < n + 1 }, n ∈ N0,

to a vertex, and join two such vertices when the closures of the corresponding components meet. Each collapsed component has diameter at most two, so the resulting rooted tree is quasi-isometric to Xl. In the two-sided model, arbitrarily many binary splits can occur during one unit of time. Indeed, for every h, there is positive probability that all edges in the first h

generations have length less than 1 /h . The set of possible arities in the coarse tree is therefore unbounded. Consequently, Theorem 7.2 also compares a structured family of unbounded-arity random trees. It does not give a classification for general offspring laws with infinite support. 

Quantising. The one-sided family is quantised on the half-line N0, as in the chain model. The two-sided family instead uses the path on Z. The following estimate supplies the only additional probabilistic input.

.The quasi-isometries in (1) and (2) can be chosen to be root-preserving. Proof of Theorem 7.2. We first prove the two positive statements. Fix an integer D ⩾ 2, put 

a = e−λ and c = λ/λ ′, and define 

hk = 1 + c(Dk − 1) (k ⩾ 0) .

Quantise a length from the first realisation by the intervals [ Dk, D k+1 ), and a length from the second by [ hk, h k+1 ). For the one-sided laws, both quantised labels have masses 

pk = aDk −1 − aDk+1 −1 (k ⩾ 0) .

This is the law p(D) in Lemma 9.6, with θ1 = a. Hence Lemma 13.1 shows that its potential on the path N0 tends to zero. For the two-sided laws, use the same intervals above 1, give their labels the indices k ⩾ 0, and use the reciprocal intervals below 1, with label −k − 1 assigned to (D−k−1, D −k] and (h−1

> k+1

, h −1 

> k

]in the first and second realisations, respectively. Both label fields then have the law q(D) of Lemma 7.1. Its potential on the path Z tends to zero. In either case the two quantised fields are independent, and within each field the labels are independent and identically distributed. For all sufficiently large D, Theorem 5.1 gives a matching automorphism with probability at least 1 − 16 ηD, where ηD → 0 is the relevant potential. Put m = min(1 , c ), M = max(1 , c ), and ρ = M/m . The cut points satisfy 

mD k ⩽ hk ⩽ M D k (k ⩾ 0) .

Thus labels at distance at most one correspond to lengths comparable within the factor ρD 2, both for the direct intervals and their reciprocals. On the matching event, extending the matching automorphism linearly along edges gives a root-preserving ρD 2-bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism, by summing the edgewise bounds along geodesics. The event that the two spaces are quasi-isometric consequently has probability at least 1 − 16 ηD for every sufficiently large D. Letting D → ∞ 

proves Items (1) and (2). It remains to separate the two families. For a proper weighted binary tree Xl, equip its end boundary with the visual metric 

dl(ξ, ζ ) = exp (−dXl (∅, ξ ∧ ζ)).

Every edge of X+ 

> λ

has length at least one. Each boundary ball is therefore a cylinder, and its two child cylinders have diameter at most e−1 times its diameter. They cover the original ball by balls of half its radius, so the boundary of every realisation of X+ 

> λ

is doubling. We next show that the boundary of X± 

> λ′

is almost surely not doubling. For each n ⩾ 1, put 

εn = (log 2) /(2 n). At any fixed vertex, the event that all edge lengths in the complete binary subtree of height n are below εn has positive probability. Taking these events over an infinite antichain gives independent trials, so such a subtree occurs almost surely for every n.Let v be the root of one of these subtrees and put ρ = exp( −dXl (∅, v )). The cylinder at v is a boundary ball of radius ρ. Choose one ray through each of the 2 n vertices at depth n below v.20 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Any two chosen rays separate at distance at most nε n = (log 2) /2 from v, so their boundary distance is at least ρ/ √2 > ρ/ 2. Hence the cylinder cannot be covered by fewer than 2 n balls of radius ρ/ 2. Since this holds for every n, the boundary is not doubling. Finally, the two-sided weighted tree is almost surely proper. Indeed, choose r > 0 so small that 2 P(L < r ) < 1. An infinite ray of finite length would eventually use only edges of length less than r, whereas the clusters of such edges are dominated by a subcritical binary Galton–Watson process. Almost surely no such ray exists. K˝ onig’s infinity lemma then shows that every bounded ball meets only finitely many branch vertices, and hence is compact. A quasi-isometry between the two proper metric trees would induce a quasisymmetry between their visual boundaries, as in the proof of Corollary 1.5. Quasisymmetries preserve doubling, which contradicts the boundary properties above and proves Item (3). □

8. Further directions 

Possible extensions. The classification settles the bounded-support problem: extinct reali-sations form the finite class, while Theorem 1.2 classifies every infinite case. It leaves several natural extensions. (1) Unbounded offspring support. The unit-time coarse graining in §7 shows that the matching theorem already applies to some structured random trees with unbounded arity. For arbitrary offspring laws, whether an exponential offspring tail suffices remains open. A complementary problem is to identify a heavy-tailed law whose realisations are not quasi-isometric to any bounded-support realisation. (2) Critical and size-biased trees. For a critical law, the local weak limit under conditioning on survival to generation n is Kesten’s size-biased tree. Its distinguished spine and critical bushes produce a geometry unlike the supercritical realisations studied here and suggest different coarse invariants. One may ask whether two independent realisations admit a natural equivalence after a scale-dependent rescaling. (3) Fractal percolation without strong separation. The proof of Corollary 3.1 uses strong separation to identify the random limit set bi-Lipschitzly with the visual boundary of its Galton–Watson coding tree. In Mandelbrot percolation, retained cubes may share boundary points, so the coding map need not be injective and Euclidean distances are not controlled by common-prefix length. The retained cubes still have a Galton–Watson genealogy, but this tree does not record their horizontal adjacencies. A ringed tree records these adjacencies by joining neighbouring retained cubes at each level. It is open whether two independent nonempty realisations of Mandelbrot percolation with the same parameters are almost surely quasisymmetrically equivalent. More generally, one may ask the same question for general fractal percolation without strong separation. (4) Continuum random trees. The Brownian-tree problem discussed above asks whether two independent Brownian continuum random trees are almost surely quasisymmetrically equivalent. Their non-doubling geometry [51] prevents the discrete bounded-degree reduction used here, but the comparison between independent realisations suggests looking for a continuum analogue of the matching principle. (5) Random higher rank. Li–Yu–Zheng [34] find rigidity for random subsets of products of regular trees. It remains to understand which products or random subspaces retain the rank-one flexibility of Theorem 1.2, and which inherit higher-rank rigidity. Part 2. Quasi-isometry classification for offspring distributions supported on {0, 1, 2}

9. Quasi-isometries through chains 

Binary branching. We first consider only offspring distributions θ that are fully supported on 

{1, 2}, i.e. those with θ1 + θ2 = 1 and 0 < θ 1 < 1. The case θ1 = 1 gives the deterministic ray, and the case θ1 = 0 gives the deterministic binary tree, making the conclusions of Theorem 1.4 trivial in those settings. 9.1. The chain encoding. Under the assumption θ1 + θ2 = 1, 0 < θ 1 < 1, the random tree 

T (ω) ⊆ N consists of binary branch points connected by chains of single-descendant vertices. Collapsing the chains encodes the random tree T by a binary tree with a random labelling, where the values at each node are independent, identically distributed geometric θ2-random variables. Formally, given v ∈ N , define κ(v) = κ(v, ω ) to be the least k ⩾ 1 such that χv1k−1 = 2, where 1k = 11 · · · 1 ( k times). If no such k exists set κ(v) = ∞. Since θ2 > 0, the first-child ray 

v, v 1, v 11 , . . . from each v ∈ N almost surely contains a vertex with offspring count 2, with 

P(κ(v) = k) = θk−11 θ2.

To make this more formal in a way that will be useful for us when we generalise this construction, note that since N is countable and θ1 + θ2 = 1, the event Ω0 = {ω ∈ Ω : χv(ω) ∈ { 1, 2} and κ(v, ω ) < ∞ for every v ∈ N } 

has full probability. Conditioning on this full probability event Ω 0, we define the initial vertex map ι : B × Ω0 → N , which locates the initial vertex in T of the chain associated to the vertex 

w ∈ B and a realisation ω ∈ Ω0. Suppressing the argument ω, ι(∅) = ∅ and, for w ∈ B and 

j ∈ { 1, 2},

ι(wj ) = ι(w)1 κ(ι(w)) −1j. (9.1) The labelling λ : B × Ω0 → N is formally defined by 

λ(w) = κ(ι(w)) . (9.2) To reach ι(wj ) from ι(w), we follow the chain to its terminating branch point and then take its jth child. Thus d(ι(w), ι (wj )) = λ(w): the number of vertices in the chain is the distance to the start of the child chains. The labelled binary tree ( B, λ ) determines T (ω), see Figure 5. Unfolding (9.1) gives 

ι(w1 · · · wn) = 1 k0 w1 1k1 w2 · · · 1kn−1 wn, kq = λ(w1 · · · wq) − 1, (9.3) where the empty prefix w1 · · · w0 is read as the root ∅. In particular, ι(w) is a function of the labels at the strict prefixes of w. The chain associated to w ∈ B is given by 

{ι(w)1 l : 0 ⩽ l ⩽ λ(w) − 1}.

It begins at ι(w), ends at the branch point ι(w)1 λ(w)−1, and carries λ(w) vertices. By construction, it is clear that these chains partition the tree and that their first-child rays are separated. We record these observations about the decomposition of T and the distribution of the labels as a pair of lemmas for later reference. 

Lemma 9.1. Let ω ∈ Ω0.(i) Every v ∈ T (ω) has a unique representation v = ι(w)1 l with w ∈ B and 0 ⩽ l ⩽ λ(w) − 1:the tree is the disjoint union of the chains. (ii) Let w, w ′ ∈ B and suppose that w is not a prefix of w′. Then the ray {ι(w)1 j : j ⩾ 0} is disjoint from the chain of w′.

Lemma 9.2. The random variables λ(w), w ∈ B, are independent and geometrically distributed:

We now compare trees associated with arbitrary positive integer labellings. A labelling of B is a function λ : B → N. Applying the recursion (9.1) to a labelling, ι(∅) = ∅ and ι(wj ) = ι(w)1 λ(w)−1j, defines the associated tree

> w∈B

with the metric inherited from N . Conditioned on Ω 0, the Galton–Watson tree is the tree associated with its label field by Lemma 9.1. The chain-separation argument uses only the recursion and positivity of the labels, so every vertex of an associated tree has unique coordinates

Lemma 9.3. Let π ∈ Aut( B) and let λ be a labelling with associated tree T . The tree T ′

associated with the labelling λ′(w) = λ(π−1w) admits a root-preserving isometry from T .

Label fields and quasi-isometries. The key deterministic step converts multiplicative compa-rability of the two label fields into a quasi-isometry of the encoded trees. 

Lemma 9.4 (Transfer lemma) . Let λ, λ ′ be labellings of B with values in N and let D ⩾ 1 satisfy 

D−1λ′(w) ⩽ λ(w) ⩽ D λ ′(w) for all w ∈ B.

Then there exists a root-preserving (D + 3) -quasi-isometry ψ : T → T ′ between the associated trees. Proof. We write ι′ for the chain-start map of λ′ and define 

ϕw(l) = ⌊l λ′(w) − 1

λ(w) ⌋ (0 ⩽ l ⩽ λ(w)),ψ(ι(w)1 l) = ι′(w)1 ϕw (l) (0 ⩽ l < λ (w)).

Unique chain coordinates make ψ well defined, and ϕ∅(0) = 0 gives root preservation. The floor inequalities give lλ ′(w)/λ (w) − 2 < ϕ w(l) ⩽ lλ ′(w)/λ (w), so the label comparison yields 

ϕw(l) ⩽ D l and l ⩽ D ϕ w(l) + 2 D. (9.4) Subtracting from λ′(w) gives the corresponding bounds for the distance to the start of either child chain: 

λ′(w) − ϕw(l) ⩽ D(λ(w) − l) + 2 and λ(w) − l ⩽ D(λ′(w) − ϕw(l)). (9.5) Segments between successive chain starts have lengths λ(v), so their lengths compare term by term within factor D, without additive error. For two points in one chain, ϕw(l + a) − ϕw(l) ∈

[ϕw(a), ϕ w(a) + 1], and (9.4) applies to their separation a. For points in different chains, the corresponding geodesics consist of complete segments and either a tail and a head, or two heads THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 23 

and the two unit edges leaving their common branch point. Applying Equations (9.4) and (9.5) to the endpoint pieces and summing gives 

d(ψ(x), ψ (y)) ⩽ D d (x, y ) + 2 and d(x, y ) ⩽ D d (ψ(x), ψ (y)) + 4 D. (9.6) The two unit edges contribute no additional error because D ⩾ 1. Finally, ϕw(0) = 0, ϕw(λ(w)) = λ′(w) − 1, and successive values of ϕw differ by at most D + 1. This also bounds the gap from the last image vertex to the target chain endpoint, so ψ(T ) is (D + 1)-dense in T ′. Dividing the reverse inequality in (9.6) by D gives an additive error bounded by 4. Since D, 4 and D + 1 are all at most D + 3, the map ψ is a ( D + 3)-quasi-isometry. □

9.3. Quantisation into level classes. We group chain lengths into classes so that lengths in neighbouring classes are multiplicatively comparable. For an integer D ⩾ 2, define the level map 

ℓD : N → N0 and its classes by 

ℓD(n) = ⌊log D n⌋, Ck = {n ∈ N : Dk ⩽ n < D k+1 } (k ∈ N0).

|ℓD(m) − ℓD(m′)| ⩽ 1, then 

m < D ℓD (m)+1 ⩽ DℓD (m′)+2 ⩽ D2m′.

The chain lengths of the second law are independent by Lemma 9.2. Applying this construction with independent uniform variables, also independent of those lengths, gives independent class labels with the same law p(D). The cut-point bounds retain the length comparisons needed for the transfer argument. 10. Allowing extinction: matching shapes 

The chain encoding rested on two features of the two-value family: every realisation is infinite, and every vertex has an infinite line of descent. Both fail for θ0 > 0. A realisation is now finite with positive probability, and any two finite trees are quasi-isometric, being bounded metric spaces. The classification therefore conditions on the family tree having infinite diameter. The vertices with infinite progeny then form the surviving skeleton , and the remaining vertices form THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 25 

finite subtrees hanging off it, the bushes. A branch point of the realisation need not be a branch point of the skeleton, so collapsing the chains of the realisation encodes the wrong tree. Instead, we collapse the chains of the skeleton and replace their integer lengths by shapes . Each shape records one skeleton chain, including its terminating branch point, together with the bushes attached along it, see Figure 6. We first describe the surviving skeleton, its bushes and the resulting shape law. We then quantise the shapes in §10.2 and show how comparisons of corresponding shapes give quasi-isometries of their assemblies in §10.3. Throughout this section θ is supported on {0, 1, 2} and supercritical with θ0 > 0, so that 

θ2 > θ 0 > 0. We write P∗ = P( · | diam T (ω) = ∞) for the law conditioned on having infinite diameter. 10.1. Shapes and the Harris decomposition. A vertex of T (ω) is a skeleton vertex if its subtree is infinite, a split if it has two children and both are skeleton vertices, and a neck vertex if it is a skeleton vertex with exactly one skeleton child. The finite subtrees rooted at the remaining children are the bushes . Conditioned on the tree having infinite diameter, the skeleton is again Galton–Watson. Elementary calculations give its offspring law and, conditioned on the skeleton, the law of its bushes, which are independent subcritical trees. For offspring supported on {0, 1, 2} both laws are explicit, and the skeleton falls in the chain regime, so the machinery of §9.1 applies to it verbatim. The decomposition is classical, due to Harris [27] and Sevastyanov [49], and we refer the reader to Athreya–Ney [7] and Lyons–Peres [36] for standard accounts. It holds for any supercritical offspring law. We state and prove the case of offspring supported on {0, 1, 2} because the later sections use its constants explicitly. A tilde marks a quantity of the skeleton, the skeleton law ˜θ

and its chain labels ˜λ, and a dagger marks the conjugate law of the bushes. 

Proposition 10.1 (Harris decomposition) . Let θ be supported on {0, 1, 2} and supercritical with 

θ0 > 0, and let T (ω) be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ. Then T (ω) is finite with probability θ0/θ 2. Conditioned on T (ω) having infinite diameter, the following hold: (i) the skeleton is a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution 

> ˜

θ1 = θ1 + 2 θ0, ˜θ2 = θ2 − θ0,

which is a two-value law, ˜θ1 + ˜θ2 = 1 , lying in the chain regime: supercriticality gives 

θ2 > θ 0, and hence 0 < ˜θ1 < 1;(ii) conditionally on the skeleton, the skeleton vertices are decorated independently. A split has no dying child, and so carries no bush. A neck vertex has a second, dying child with probability 2θ0/(θ1 + 2 θ0) and no second child with the complementary probability 

θ1/(θ1 + 2 θ0); a dying child is the root of a bush, which is an independent Galton–Watson tree with the conjugate law 

θ† = ( θ†

> 0

Write q for the extinction probability, which is the root in [0 , 1) of s = θ0 + θ1s + θ2s2.Rearranging and factoring gives ( s − 1)( θ2s − θ0) = 0, so q = θ0/θ 2. A fixed child founds a surviving subtree with probability 1 − q, independently of its siblings. A vertex whose subtree is infinite has either one child which survives, two children of which exactly one survives, or two children which both survive. Their respective weights are θ1(1 − q), 2 θ2q(1 − q) and θ2(1 − q)2.These sum to 1 − q, the probability of survival, so they exhaust the conditioning. Hence 

P(two survive | some survive) = θ2(1 − q)2

1 − q = θ2(1 − q) = θ2 − θ0.

Figure 6. A bushy realisation conditioned on having infinite diameter, and its shape labelling. Filled vertices form the surviving skeleton and thick edges its chains. Hollow vertices form the bushes. The dashed regions are the shapes: each runs from a child of a split, or from the root, to the next split, and collects the bushes of its neck vertices. Assembling the shape-labelled binary tree recovers the realisation up to isometry. A child conditioned to die founds a Galton–Watson tree with the law tilted by the extinction probability, θ†

Conditioned on the realisation having infinite diameter, the skeleton is a Galton–Watson tree with the offspring law computed above [36, Proposition 5.28]. The conditional independence of the decorations follows from the embedding into a two-type branching process, one type the vertices with infinite line of descent and the other the vertices with finitely many descendants [7, Chapter I.12]. □

We encode the skeleton as in §9.1 but with ˜θ in place of θ. Its chains are indexed by B, and under

Definition 10.2 (Shapes) . A shape is a pair σ = ( m, (b1, . . . , b m−1)), where m ⩾ 1 and each bi

is either empty or a finite tree with offspring numbers in {0, 1, 2}. Its realisation is the finite rooted tree obtained from a path v1 · · · vm by attaching, for each nonempty bi, the root of bi by an edge at vi. The entry is v1 and the exit is vm. The size |σ| is the number of vertices of the realisation, and we write S to denote the (countable) set of all shapes. 

Assembling shapes. We write S(w) for the shape formed by the skeleton chain indexed by w,including its terminating split and the bushes at its preceding neck vertices. A family of shapes indexed by B is assembled into a tree by joining, for every w ∈ B, the exit of the shape at w to the entries of the shapes at w1 and w2 by an edge, and rooting the result at the entry of the shape at ∅.

Proposition 10.3 (Shape decomposition) . Conditioned on the tree having infinite diameter, the shapes S(w), w ∈ B, are i.i.d. with the law μ of (m, (B1, . . . , B m−1)) , where P(m = k) = ˜θ k−11 ˜θ2

and, given m, the decorations B1, . . . , B m−1 are independent, each empty with probability θ1/˜θ1

and otherwise an independent Galton–Watson tree with law θ†. Moreover T (ω) is isometric to the assembly of (S(w)) w∈B.THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 27 

Proof. We use the independent geometric chain labels described above, obtained from Proposi-tion 10.1(i) and Lemma 9.2. The chains partition the skeleton by Lemma 9.1. Conditioned on the skeleton, the decorations at the neck vertices are independent, each nonempty with probability 2θ0/˜θ1 by Proposition 10.1(ii). The shape S(w) is the pair of ˜λ(w) and the decorations at the ˜λ(w) − 1 neck vertices of the chain of w. Distinct chains are disjoint, so, conditioned on the skeleton, the decoration families of distinct shapes are independent, and the conditional law of the decorations of S(w) depends on the skeleton only through ˜λ(w). Together with the independence of the labels, this shows that the shapes are i.i.d. with the stated law. Finally, the skeleton chains together with their bushes partition the vertices of T (ω), and the chain of w

with its bushes realises S(w). The edges between consecutive shapes are exactly the skeleton edges from a split to its skeleton children, so identifying each shape with its realisation gives an isometry from the assembly to T (ω). □

Shape mass bounds. Let S be a shape with law μ. We give an exponential upper bound for the probability that S is large and an exponential lower bound for the mass of each shape with positive probability. 

Lemma 10.4 (Shape mass bounds) . There are c, C, n 0 > 0, depending on θ, such that (i) μ(|S| ⩾ n) ⩽ e−cn for all n ⩾ n0, and (ii) μ(σ) ⩾ e−C|σ| for every σ ∈ S with μ(σ) > 0.Proof. For Item (ii), fix σ = ( m, (b1, . . . , b m−1)) with μ(σ) > 0. The geometric chain probability contributes m factors, the decoration choices contribute m − 1, and each bush vertex contributes one offspring factor. Thus μ(σ) is a product of at most 2 |σ| factors, each of them one of ˜θ1, ˜θ2,

θ1/˜θ1, 2 θ0/˜θ1, or an offspring weight θ† 

> j

of a bush vertex. Let p be the least positive number among these seven constants. Since μ(σ) > 0, every factor is at least p ∈ (0 , 1], so μ(σ) ⩾ p2|σ|.With C = 2 log p−1 this is e−C|σ|.For Item (i), the Chernoff bound for the offspring sums in a tree exploration gives a finite exponential moment for the total progeny Z under the subcritical law θ†, whose support is bounded. By Proposition 10.3, |S| is stochastically dominated by m + Z1 + · · · + Zm−1, with m

geometric and the Zi independent copies of Z independent of m, so Eet|S| < ∞ for some t > 0. Another Chernoff bound gives μ(|S| ⩾ n) ⩽ (Eet|S|)e−tn ⩽ e−tn/ 2 for all sufficiently large n. □

10.2. Net quantisation of shape classes. We compare shapes through maps that control their entries and exits, where the shapes join in an assembled quasi-isometry. A K-marked quasi-isometry σ → τ is a K-quasi-isometry of the realisations mapping the entry of σ within distance K of the entry of τ , and likewise for the exits. Two shapes are K-comparable if a 

K-marked quasi-isometry exists in at least one of the two directions. Comparability at a fixed scale is reflexive and symmetric but need not be transitive, so we build the classes as a net around representatives. 

Definition 10.5 (The net and the label graph) . Fix an integer D ⩾ 2 and enumerate S by size. If two shapes have the same size, enumerate them arbitrarily. We process this enumeration in order, retaining a shape precisely when it admits no D-marked quasi-isometry to an earlier retained shape. We write RD ⊆ S for the set of retained shapes. For σ ∈ S , let rep D(σ) be the earliest member of RD to which σ admits a D-marked quasi-isometry. The label graph GD has vertex set RD, with an edge between distinct σ∗, τ ∗ ∈ R D whenever they are 27 D4-comparable. 

Measurability. Since S is countable, rep D and every event built from it below are measurable. 

Lemma 10.6 (Basic net properties) . The composition of a K-marked and a K′-marked quasi-isometry is 3KK ′-marked, and every K-marked quasi-isometry has a 3K2-marked quasi-inverse. Every shape σ ∈ S has a representative rep D(σ). For each σ∗ ∈ R D, we have rep D(σ∗) = σ∗,so every class rep −1 

> D

(σ∗) contains its representative. The size bound |rep D(σ)| ⩽ |σ| holds for every σ ∈ S .Any two members of one class admit a 9D3-marked quasi-isometry. Members of equal or adjacent classes are 729 D7-comparable. 28 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Proof. The bounds 3 KK ′ and 3 K2 follow from Definition 2.1 and the triangle inequality, choosing a quasi-inverse f − of a K-marked map f with d(f (f −(y)) , y ) ⩽ K. The greedy construction gives every shape a representative no later in the size enumeration and fixes retained shapes, proving all assertions about rep D.Composing through the common representative gives a marked quasi-isometry between members of one class with constant 3 D(3 D2) = 9 D3. For adjacent classes, exchange the shapes if necessary to orient the 27 D4-marked map from the first representative to the second. Composing the D-marked map to the first representative, this map, and a 3 D2-marked quasi-inverse from the second representative to the second shape gives the constant 9 D(27 D4)(3 D2) = 729 D7. □

Connectivity. Although we do not use connectivity below, we can see it by deleting a leaf. Given a representative σ∗ of size at least two, delete a leaf from a nonempty bush, or the exit if the shape is a bare path, to obtain a shape τ of size |σ∗| − 1. Collapsing the deleted vertex onto its neighbour gives a 1-marked quasi-isometry σ∗ → τ . Composing with τ → rep D(τ ) gives a 3D-marked map by Lemma 10.6, so σ∗ is adjacent to the strictly smaller representative rep D(τ ). Iterating connects every vertex to the singleton representative, proving the following lemma. 

Lemma 10.7 (Connectivity of the label graph) . The graph GD is connected. 

Counting representatives. We next bound the number of representatives below a given size. The factor D−1/3 in the bound records the gain from allowing comparisons at scale D.

Lemma 10.8 (Entropy dilution) . There is an absolute constant c0 such that for all integers 

D ⩾ 2 and n ⩾ 1, every family of shapes of size at most n, no two of which admit a D-marked quasi-isometry, has at most exp (c0(nD −1/3 + 1) ) members. In particular, there are at most 

exp (c0(nD −1/3 + 1) ) representatives of size at most n.Proof. The Catalan bound of 4 k−1 for rooted plane trees with k vertices [23, Section I.5.1] gives at most ∑Kk=1 k4k−1 ⩽ e3K rooted trees with at most K vertices and one additional marked vertex, up to isomorphism. For D ⩽ 729, both counts are at most e3n ⩽ e27 nD −1/3

, since the marked realisations have at most n vertices and a marked isometry gives a D-marked map in either direction. For D > 729, set s = ⌊(D/ 72) 1/3⌋ ⩾ 2, so that 72 s3 ⩽ D and s−1 ⩽ 9D−1/3. We cut the realisation of a shape σ into connected parts by passing upwards from the leaves. Each vertex combines the remainders of its children with itself, cutting off a part and passing up nothing as soon as the combined size reaches s. Since every remainder has fewer than s vertices and each vertex has at most two children, every cut part has between s and 2 s − 1 vertices. For |σ| ⩽ n,the disjoint cut parts and the possible smaller remainder at the root therefore form at most 

n/s + 1 parts. Contract them to obtain T (σ), rooted at the part of the entry and marked at the part of the exit, and let P be the projection. A path in the contraction lifts with at most 2 s − 2steps inside each part, giving 

d(P (x), P (y)) ⩽ d(x, y ) ⩽ (2 s − 1) d(P (x), P (y)) + 2 s − 2.

Thus P is a surjective 2 s-marked quasi-isometry preserving both marks exactly. If T (σ) and T (τ ) are isomorphic as rooted marked trees, composing the projections and their quasi-inverses through the isomorphism gives marked quasi-isometries in both directions with constant 3 · 2s · 3(2 s)2 = 72 s3 ⩽ D, by Lemma 10.6. Hence distinct family members have nonisomorphic marked contractions, as do distinct representatives, since a map from a later representative to an earlier one is excluded by the greedy construction. Counting the contractions gives both bounds with c0 = 27, since 

e3( n/s +1) ⩽ e27 nD −1/3+3 ⩽ e27( nD −1/3+1) . □

10.3. Glued transfer. We combine marked quasi-isometries between corresponding shapes by defining the map separately on each copy. A path between two copies traverses each intermediate chain together with a joining edge. These traversals have positive length, so their additive errors can be absorbed into a multiplicative bound. 11. Matching independent graph labels on the binary tree 

We now prove Theorem 5.1 for independent, identically distributed labellings. At each split, an automorphism can either preserve or exchange the two child subtrees. We control this recursive pairing by a potential that contracts when it is small. For full labellings, the matching must also respect the labels at the root, so we need a second estimate for combining independent conditions. 11.1. Setup and the compatibility rule. We recall the notation of Sections 2.1 and 5. For 

h ⩾ 0, the complete binary tree Bh consists of the words over {1, 2} of length at most h, with leaves {1, 2}h. An automorphism π ∈ Aut( Bh) fixes the root, preserves levels and recursively fixes or interchanges the two child subtrees at each internal vertex. 

Compatibility. The label graph G = ( V, E ) is countable and undirected and μ is a probability measure on V . We allow disconnected G, taking distances between components to be infinite, as in Remark 11.1. We say that two labels v, w ∈ V are compatible when 

dG(v, w ) ⩽ 1, (11.1) and write BG(v, 1) = {w ∈ V : dG(v, w ) ⩽ 1} for the closed unit ball. The relation (11.1) is symmetric and reflexive, the only two properties used by the estimates. 30 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT

Lemma 11.4 (2 × 2 transversal) . Mark each cell of a 2 × 2 array good or bad. Remark 11.5 . The numerical constants in Theorem 5.1 are convenient uniform choices rather than optimised values. For the leaf estimate, we replace A5/2 and B5/2 by the rational bounds 7/8 and 29, which gives the contraction factor 253 /256 at potential 1 /256. The choices for the full matching estimate are checked in §11.4. Optimising the intermediate pointwise bounds would improve both conclusions. 

Remark 11.6 . The exponent 5 /2 is not structural. The pointwise estimates and the product step require only α ⩾ 1, while the present contraction estimate requires Aα < 1. This fails at 

α = 2, where A2 = 59 /54, and holds at α = 5 /2 by (11.18). We use 5 /2 because it gives short explicit bounds and because squaring inequalities involving a half-integer power reduces them to polynomial inequalities with rational coefficients. For full labellings, the stronger estimate in Lemma 17.1, used to prove Theorem 6.1, allows every α ⩾ 4/3, including exponents below 2, with an α-dependent constant and smallness threshold. Smaller exponents reduce the potential at the cost of the constant in the matching bound as well as the admissible threshold. THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 35 

11.3. A product lemma. For full labellings, matching the two child subtrees must be combined with compatibility at the root. These conditions involve independent labels, so we need a product estimate for their potentials. 

Lemma 11.7 (Product of relations) . Let (Xi, μ i, R i), i = 1 , 2, be countable probability spaces with symmetric reflexive relations, and let R1 ⊗ R2 be the relation on X1 × X 2 defined by 

(x1, x 2) R1 ⊗ R2 (y1, y 2) ⇐⇒ x1 R1 y1 and x2 R2 y2.

With Φi := Φ( Ri, μ i) and any α ⩾ 1,

Φ( R1 ⊗ R2, μ 1 ⊗ μ2) ⩽ Φ1 + Φ 2 + 2 α Φ1Φ2. (11.34) 

Proof. Fix x = ( x1, x 2) ∈ supp μ1 × supp μ2, and let qi be the bad degree of xi in Ri and put 

ri = 1 − qi. Under R1 ⊗ R2, the good degree of x is r1r2, so its bad degree is q = q1 + (1 − q1)q2.Thus (11.11) gives 

ϕα(q) ⩽ ϕα(q1) + ϕα(q2) + 2 αϕ α(q1)ϕα(q2).

Taking expectations over the independent coordinates X1 ∼ μ1 and X2 ∼ μ2 gives (11.34). □

Infinite tree. Matches chosen separately at each finite height need not agree under restriction. We use compactness to obtain a compatible sequence of them. The sets Fh ⊆ Aut( Bh) of matching automorphisms are nonempty and finite, and each πh+1 ∈ F h+1 restricts to a unique parent in Fh. This is an infinite, finitely-branching, rooted tree, so K˝ onig’s infinity lemma provides a compatible branch (πh)h⩾0, whose common extension is an automorphism of the infinite tree matching every vertex. The claim gives both the measurability of M and P(M ) ⩾ 1 − KΦ0. □
