13. Matching and quasi-isometry 

We now combine the matching theorem with the chain and shape constructions to prove the quasi-isometry classification for offspring distributions supported on {0, 1, 2}. Matching supplies compatible labels, and the local comparison and transfer results turn them into quasi-isometries. 13.1. The potential of the quantised law. The quantised labels of §9.3 are independent and identically distributed on N0 with the law p(D). We compare them on the path P of §12.1, so two labels are compatible when they differ by at most one. Theorem 5.1 then applies once the potential ηP,5/2(p(D)) falls below 10 −4. By Lemma 9.6, the class 0 carries all but θD−11 of the mass, and class j carries at most θDj −11 for j ⩾ 1. Thus the masses decay doubly exponentially in the class index. For large D, each class away from the origin has a heavier preceding class. The estimate below uses this compatible mass to control the denominator in the potential. 

Lemma 13.1 (Chain potential bound) . For every θ1 ∈ (0 , 1) there exists D0 = D0(θ1) ⩾ 5 such that for every integer D ⩾ D0 the law p(D) of Lemma 9.6 satisfies 

ηP,5/2

13.2. Proof of Theorem 1.4. The encoding of §9.1 turns each of the two realisations into a label field on B, and by Lemma 9.6 the quantised fields are independent of each other and, within each realisation, independent and identically distributed with the law p(D). Lemma 13.1 shows that this law satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem 5.1. Thus, with probability close to one, a single automorphism of B aligns the two fields to within one class. By Lemma 9.5, this alignment makes the chain lengths comparable up to the factor D2, and Lemma 9.4 turns this comparison into a quasi-isometry of the two realisations. Taking a union over integer scales D

then gives the almost sure statement. 

Theorem 1.4 of Part 1, restated. Let θ be an offspring distribution with θ1 + θ2 = 1 , and let

Remark 13.2 (The zero–one law is not needed) . The almost sure statement does not use a zero–one law. Every ED is an explicit event contained in the event that the trees admit a root-preserving quasi-isometry, and P(ED) → 1 by (13.3). The argument requires neither monotonicity of ED in D nor the reverse containment. Indeed, a quasi-isometry of the two trees need not arise from a matching automorphism. A zero–one law is nevertheless available. Represent the tree by one of the i.i.d. fields used in this part: the chain lengths λ(w), w ∈ B, of Lemma 9.2 when θ0 = 0, or the shapes S(w), 

w ∈ B, of Proposition 10.3 when θ0 > 0 and the tree is conditioned on having infinite diameter. Exchanging the values at finitely many vertices affects the constants of any quasi-isometry but not whether a quasi-isometry exists. Applying the Hewitt–Savage zero–one law [22, Theorem 2.5.4] then gives the desired zero–one law for {T (ω) ≃ T (ω′)}.13.3. Universality in the chain regime. Two realisations drawn from different two-value laws carry label fields with different geometric parameters, so their quantised classes have different probabilities. Lemma 9.7 re-quantises the second law, using boundary randomisation with one auxiliary uniform variable per vertex, so that its classes carry the probabilities p(D) 

> k

of the first. The two quantised fields then have the same law, and we can apply the matching argument of THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 41 

§13.2. The classes of the second law meet at the cut points D′ 

> k

rather than at the powers Dk.These cut points are comparable to Dk up to a factor depending only on the two laws, which introduces an extra bounded factor in the distortion.

13.4. The bushy regime. We now consider θ supported on {0, 1, 2} and supercritical with 

θ0 > 0. Recall the shapes S(w) of Definition 10.2, the net RD with the map rep D, the label graph GD of Definition 10.5, and the law P∗ conditioned on survival from §10. We write μ for the shape law under P∗. We bound the potential of the shape labels and construct a common label law for two offspring distributions. Matching these labels and applying glued transfer will give universality within the regime. 13.4.1. The shape label law and its potential. The label of w is the class rep D(S(w)) of its shape. By Proposition 10.3 the labels are independent and identically distributed with the pushforward law μD = μ ◦ rep −1 

> D

on the vertex set RD of GD, and two of them are compatible when they are equal or adjacent in GD. Thus Theorem 5.1 applies once the potential of μD is small. To bound the potential, we shrink large shapes to smaller members of the same support. Their representatives are compatible, and each smaller shape supplies a lower bound for the compatible mass. We then group the potential sum by the sizes of the original shapes. For large D, the exponential size tail of Lemma 10.4 outweighs the growth of the reciprocal compatible mass, giving a geometric sum. This is the same comparison as in Lemma 13.1, where the geometric chain law gives double-exponential decay in the class index because the cut points grow as powers of D.We record the shrinking first, in the form that both this bound and the coupling of §13.4.2 use, in which the target support may belong to a second offspring distribution. 

Lemma 13.4 (Shrinking a shape into a support) . Let θ′ be supported on {0, 1, 2} and supercritical with θ′ 

> 0

> 0, and let μ′ be its shape law. (i) Every shape σ admits a shape σ◦ ∈ supp μ′, its support fix , with |σ◦| ⩽ 2|σ| and a 

2-marked quasi-isometry σ → σ◦, and σ◦ = σ whenever σ ∈ supp μ′.(ii) Let D ⩾ 2 be an integer and put s = ⌊√D/ 2592 ⌋. If s ⩾ 1, then every shape σ of size 

n admits a shape σ0 ∈ supp μ′ with |σ0| ⩽ 16( n/s + 1) and a D-marked quasi-isometry 

σ → σ0.We fix the choices in these constructions, so that σ 7 → σ◦ and σ 7 → σ0 are well-defined maps 

S → supp μ′.Proof. For Item (i), the shape law of Proposition 10.3 has full support when θ′ 

> 1

> 0, so we take σ◦ = σ. When θ′ 

> 1

= 0, its support consists precisely of the shapes with a bush at every neck vertex other than the exit and with zero or two children at every bush vertex. Attach a one-vertex bush at each bare neck vertex other than the exit and a leaf at each bush vertex with one child. This adds at most one vertex per original vertex, giving σ◦ ∈ supp μ′ with |σ◦| ⩽ 2|σ|,and leaves supported shapes unchanged. The inclusion preserves distances and both marks and has 1-dense image, so it is 2-marked. Collapsing the added leaves to their attachment vertices preserves both marks and changes distances by at most 2, giving a 2-marked map in the reverse direction as well. For Item (ii), apply the cut from the proof of Lemma 10.8 at scale s. The projection onto the tree T of parts is 2 s-marked, with the entry part as root and the exit part as mark. There are 

k ⩽ n/s + 1 parts, each containing at most 2 s − 1 vertices. A connected part with m vertices in a tree with at most two children per vertex has at most m + 1 outgoing child edges, so every vertex of T has at most 2 s children. We turn T into a supported shape in three steps. First, replace each vertex with d > 2 children by a spine of d − 1 vertices, attaching its children in their order so that every spine vertex has two children. Send each old vertex to the first vertex of its spine, or to itself if it is unchanged, and take the images of the root and mark as the marks of the resulting binary tree B. Collapsing the spines back to their original vertices does not increase distances, while each old edge becomes a path of length at most 2 s. Every added vertex is within 2 s of the image, so this map is 4 s-marked. At most k − 1 vertices are added, giving |B| ⩽ 2k.Second, append a new exit as a child of the marked vertex and take the path from the root to this exit as the neck. If the mark had two children, attach them through one new vertex to form a single bush. Every other neck vertex already has at most one subtree off the neck, so the THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 43 

result is a shape with at most |B| + 2 vertices. The map keeping the original vertices changes distances by at most one and has 1-dense image. It preserves the root and sends the old mark within one step of the new exit, so it is 2-marked and hence 6-marked. Third, let σ0 be the support fix of this shape. By Item (i), this step is 2-marked and at most doubles the size, so 

|σ0| ⩽ 2( |B| + 2) ⩽ 4k + 4 ⩽ 16 k ⩽ 16( n/s + 1) .

By Lemma 10.6, composing the projection and the three maps gives a marked quasi-isometry with constant 33 · 2s · 4s · 6 · 2 = 2592 s2 ⩽ D. 

The deterministic cut and fixed child order for the spines and leaf attachments make these assignments well-defined. □

13.4.2. Shape classes across two laws. Two bushy laws generally have different shape laws. To apply Theorem 5.1, we give their label fields a common law. Lemma 9.7 achieves this for two chain laws by re-cutting the second law at thresholds chosen to reproduce the class probabilities of the first. For shapes, we use a coupling of the two laws. Each shape draws a partner from the other law, and we record the pair with the same coordinate order in both labellings. The resulting labels have a common law on pairs. We require comparisons between compatible pair labels to give marked maps between the original shapes at a scale polynomial in D, while the label law has a small potential.

13.4.3. Universality in the bushy regime. Lemma 13.6 labels the two realisations by fields that are independent and identically distributed with one law on a graph of small potential. Theorem 5.1 matches the two fields along a single automorphism of B, and a matched pair of labels supplies a marked quasi-isometry between the shapes sitting at the two vertices it identifies. Lemma 10.9 glues these into a quasi-isometry of the two assemblies. 

Proposition 13.7 (Universality in the bushy regime) . Let θ and θ′ be supercritical, supported on {0, 1, 2}, with θ0, θ ′ 

> 0

> 0, and let T , T ′ be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Then almost surely there is a root-preserving quasi-isometry T → T ′. Moreover, there is D2 = D2(θ, θ ′) such that for every integer D ⩾ D2, with Q, μQ and c3 the label graph, the label law and the constant of Lemma 13.6 at scale D,

P(there is no root-preserving 8 · 972 2D8-quasi-isometry T → T ′) ⩽ 16 ηQ,5/2(μQ) ⩽ 16 e−c3D2

(13.9) 

Proof. We work with the product of the two laws conditioned on having infinite diameter and enlarge it by families ( U (w)) w∈B and ( U ′(w)) w∈B of uniform variables on [0 , 1), all independent of one another and of the two trees. Let D2 be at least the D1 of Lemma 13.6 and fix an integer 

D ⩾ D2, with Q, μQ, ℓ, ℓ′ as there. By Proposition 10.3, T is isometric to the assembly of an i.i.d. shape sequence ( S(w)) w∈B with law μ and T ′ to that of ( S′(w)) w∈B with law μ′, the two sequences independent. The label fields 

x(w) = ℓ(S(w), U (w)), y(w) = ℓ′(S′(w), U ′(w))

are therefore independent of each other and i.i.d. with the common law μQ, by Lemma 13.6(i). By Lemma 13.6(iii) the potential condition of Theorem 5.1 holds for Q, so with probability at least 1 − 16 ηQ,5/2(μQ) there is π ∈ Aut( B) with dQ

(x(πw ), y (w)) ⩽ 1 for all w ∈ B. When this occurs, Lemma 13.6(ii) provides a 972 D4-marked quasi-isometry S(πw ) → S′(w) for every w.The assembly of ( S(πw )) w∈B is isometric to that of ( S(w)) w∈B, since carrying the copy of S(πw )THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 47 

at w identically to its copy at πw respects the gluing, π mapping the children of a word to the children of its image. Hence Lemma 10.9, applied with K = 972 D4, yields a root-preserving 8 · 972 2D8-quasi-isometry T → T ′. The identifications with the assemblies send each root to the entry of the root copy, and the relabelling isometry preserves this entry because π fixes the root. The event of successful label matching is therefore contained in the event that such a quasi-isometry exists. This latter event depends only on the original trees, so its probability is unchanged on passing to their conditioned product law. This proves (13.9), its second inequality being Lemma 13.6(iii). As at the end of the proof of Theorem 1.4, letting D → ∞ through integers in (13.9) gives the almost sure statement. □

13.5. Quasi-isometry classification for offspring supported on {0, 1, 2}. The pieces of this part assemble into the classification restricted to offspring distributions supported on {0, 1, 2}.

Theorem 13.8 (Quasi-isometry classification for offspring supported on {0, 1, 2}). Let θ =(θ0, θ 1, θ 2) and θ′ = ( θ′

> 0

, θ ′

> 1

, θ ′

> 2

) be offspring distributions, each either supercritical or concentrated at 1, and let T , T ′ be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions, conditioned on having infinite diameter. The classes of Theorem 1.2 take the form (R) θ1 = 1 , in which T is the ray. (F) θ0 = θ1 = 0 , so that θ2 = 1 and T is the binary tree. 

(C ⟨1⟩) θ0 = 0 < θ 1 < 1, the two-value family θ1 + θ2 = 1 of Theorem 1.4. (B) θ0 > 0, where supercriticality implies θ2 > θ 0.If θ and θ′ belong to the same class, then almost surely there is a root-preserving quasi-isometry 

T → T ′. If they belong to different classes, then almost surely T̸ ≃ T ′. In particular, almost every realisation from a law outside class (R) is not quasi-isometric to the ray. 

This is the case J = 2 of Theorem 1.2, with all four infinite regimes present. The branching semigroup does not further split the chain regime. Indeed, a chain-regime law supported on 

{0, 1, 2} gives weight to the arity 2 and no other arity above one, so Λ θ = ⟨1⟩ = N0. The chain regime is therefore the single class (C ⟨1⟩) here. The positive conclusions follow from the deterministic ray and binary tree cases and from Propositions 13.3 and 13.7. We now separate the different classes by their geometry. 

Rays and bushes. Almost surely, a realisation in class (F) or (C ⟨1⟩) has every vertex below its first branch point on a geodesic line, and the remaining vertices lie in a bounded set near the root. A realisation in class (B) almost surely carries bushes of unbounded depth, each cut off from the rest of the tree by a single vertex. A vertex at the bottom of a deep bush is far from every line. The first proposition below separates these cases. The second uses three rays from a common vertex to separate all three non-ray classes from the ray. We then use long chains to separate the chain class from the binary tree, giving Theorem 13.14. 

Lines. A line (or bi-infinite geodesic ) in a tree T is a subset isometric to Z, equivalently the union of two rays from a common vertex leaving it through distinct neighbours. A bush of depth 

h attached at a vertex c is a finite component P of T \ { c} with P ⊆ BT (c, h ) containing a vertex at distance h from c. Removing a vertex of P leaves components that lie inside P , hence are bounded, together with one component containing c. Thus no vertex of a bush lies on a line, and bushes of unbounded depth contain vertices arbitrarily far from every line. 

Separation. Both separation proofs below use the following elementary form of geodesic stability in a tree, whose proof is standard but which we include for completeness. A more general statement, for quasi-geodesics in a Gromov hyperbolic space, is [16, Theorem III.H.1.7]. 

Lemma 13.9 (Stability in a tree) . Let T and T ′ be trees, let ψ : T → T ′ be a D-quasi-isometry, and let x, y ∈ T . Every vertex of the geodesic [ψ(x), ψ (y)] lies within distance D of the image under ψ of the geodesic [x, y ].Proof. Write x = z0, z 1, . . . , z n = y for the vertices of [ x, y ] in order. Consecutive ones are adjacent, so d′(ψ(zi), ψ (zi+1 )) ⩽ D + D = 2 D. Let m lie on [ ψ(x), ψ (y)]. If m = ψ(zi) for some i, the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise every image avoids m. Removing m separates 

ψ(x) from ψ(y), and ψ(z0) and ψ(zn) therefore lie in different components of T ′ \ { m}, so 48 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

some consecutive pair ψ(zi), ψ (zi+1 ) does too. The geodesic joining that pair passes through 

m, whence d′(ψ(zi), m ) + d′(m, ψ (zi+1 )) ⩽ 2D and one of the two summands is at most D, as required. □

Proposition 13.10 (Bushes against lines) . Let T and T ′ be trees. Suppose that T has bushes of unbounded depth and that every vertex of T ′ lies within distance r of a line of T ′. Then T̸ ≃ T ′.Proof. Suppose ψ : T → T ′ is a D-quasi-isometry. Let P be a bush of depth h > 2D2 + Dr at a vertex c, with x ∈ P at distance h from c. Write w = ψ(c) and y = ψ(x). Choose a line of T ′ within distance r of y and a vertex y′ on it with d′(y, y ′) ⩽ r. Of the two rays of the line from y′, choose one for which y′ ∈ [w, z ] for every vertex z on the ray. Take such a z with d′(y′, z ) > Dh + 2 D, and choose p ∈ T with d′(ψ(p), z ) ⩽ D. Then p / ∈ P , since otherwise d′(w, ψ (p)) ⩽ Dh + D, whereas d′(w, ψ (p)) ⩾ d′(w, z ) − D ⩾ d′(y′, z ) − D > Dh + D.Moreover, y′ ∈ [w, ψ (p)]: the common initial segment of [ w, z ] and [ w, ψ (p)] ends within distance D of z, beyond y′. Hence Lemma 13.9 gives q ∈ [c, p ] with d′(ψ(q), y ′) ⩽ D, so 

d′(ψ(q), ψ (x)) ⩽ D + r and d(q, x ) ⩽ D(D + r) + D2 = 2 D2 + Dr . On the other hand c and 

p lie outside the component P , so [ c, p ] misses P and d(q, x ) = d(q, c ) + h ⩾ h > 2D2 + Dr , a contradiction. □

Proposition 13.11 (Three rays against the ray) . Let T be a tree containing three rays that pairwise meet only in their common initial vertex. Then T is not quasi-isometric to the ray N0.Proof. Suppose ψ : T → N0 is a D-quasi-isometry and let v be the common initial vertex of three such rays. Fix an integer r > 2D2 and let a1, a 2, a 3 be the vertices at distance r from v on the three rays. Each of the three images is at least ψ(v) or at most ψ(v), so two of them lie weakly on one side of ψ(v), and of those two the nearer to ψ(v) lies on the geodesic joining ψ(v)to the further. Relabelling, ψ(a1) lies on [ ψ(v), ψ (a2)]. By Lemma 13.9 there is q on the geodesic [ v, a 2] with |ψ(q)−ψ(a1)| ⩽ D, so d(q, a 1) ⩽ D2 +D2.But [ v, a 2] is an initial segment of the second ray, which meets the first ray only at v, so every path from a1 to a vertex of [ v, a 2] passes through v and d(a1, q ) ⩾ d(a1, v ) = r > 2D2, a contradiction. □

The supercritical regimes. We now verify these geometric hypotheses almost surely in the three supercritical regimes. Class (B) supplies the bushes of unbounded depth, classes (F) and (C ⟨1⟩) supply the nearby lines, and all three contain the required rays. 

Lemma 13.12 (The regimes against the obstructions) . Let θ be supercritical and supported on 

{0, 1, 2}, and let T be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ, conditioned on having infinite diameter. (i) Almost surely T contains three rays that pairwise meet only in their common initial vertex. (ii) If θ0 = 0 , then almost surely there is a finite r such that every vertex of T lies within distance r of a line. (iii) If θ0 > 0, then almost surely T has bushes of unbounded depth. Proof. Supercriticality forces θ1 + 2 θ2 > 1 and hence θ2 > 0. Write T ∗ for T when θ0 = 0 and for its skeleton when θ0 > 0. In both cases T ∗ is a Galton–Watson tree whose offspring distribution is supported on {1, 2}, by Proposition 10.1(i) in the second case, so every vertex of T ∗ has an infinite line of descent, and T ∗ is a subtree of T carrying the induced metric. For Item (i), we exhibit the three rays in T ∗. If θ0 = θ1 = 0, then T ∗ = T = B and a child v

of the root carries them, one descending from each of the two children of v and one climbing to the root and descending into its other child. Otherwise the offspring distribution of T ∗ gives positive weight to 1, so Lemma 9.1 applies to it: the chain of ∅ ends at a branch point u, and the chain starting at the second child of u ends at a branch point v strictly below u. Descending from each of the two children of v gives two rays leaving v through distinct neighbours, and climbing from v to u and descending into the other child of u gives a third ray, leaving v through its parent. The three pairwise meet only at v.THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 49 

For Item (ii), let θ0 = 0, so that T = T ∗ and every subtree of T is infinite. If θ1 = 0, then 

T = B and every vertex lies on a line, so r = 0 works. Otherwise 0 < θ 1 < 1. Let b be the branch point ending the chain of ∅, at distance λ(∅) − 1 from the root. The two subtrees below the children of b are infinite, so b lies on a line. Let v lie strictly below b, say in the subtree below one child of b. The subtree below v is infinite, and the component of T \ { v} containing the parent of v contains the subtree below the other child of b and is infinite as well, so v lies on a line too. Every remaining vertex lies on the chain of ∅ strictly above b, at distance at most 

λ(∅) − 1 from b. So r = λ(∅) − 1 works, and it is finite almost surely by Lemma 9.2. For Item (iii), let θ0 > 0. A bush attached at a neck vertex c is joined to the rest of T by the single edge at c, so it is a component of T \ { c}, and it is finite. Its depth is the largest distance from c to one of its vertices. By Proposition 10.1(ii), conditioned on the skeleton, the neck decorations are independent. Each is nonempty with probability 2 θ0/˜θ1 > 0 and, when nonempty, is a Galton–Watson tree with conjugate law θ† = ( θ2, θ 1, θ 0). Fix h ∈ N. Since 

θ† 

> 0

= θ2 > 0 and θ† 

> 2

= θ0 > 0, the complete binary tree of height h has positive θ†-probability, so a given neck vertex carries a bush of depth exceeding h with a probability ph > 0 that does not depend on the vertex. Lemma 9.2 applied to the skeleton makes the chain lengths ˜λ(w), 

w ∈ B, independent and geometric, so almost surely infinitely many of them exceed 1 and T has infinitely many neck vertices. Conditioned on the skeleton, the decorations are independent, so the second Borel–Cantelli lemma gives, almost surely, infinitely many bushes of depth exceeding 

h. Intersecting over h ∈ N proves Item (iii), as required. □

The binary tree and the chain regime. The remaining separation within the two-value family is between the chain regime and the binary tree. Long chains create arbitrarily large balls with only linear growth, whereas balls in the binary tree grow exponentially. 

Proposition 13.13 (Bottleneck obstruction) . Let T be a tree such that for every ℓ ∈ N there is a vertex vℓ whose ball BT (vℓ, ℓ ) is isometric to a segment of Z. Then T is not quasi-isometric to B.Proof. Assume for contradiction that ψ : T → B is a D-quasi-isometry. Fix ℓ ∈ N, put v = vℓ,and let r ∈ N. We first show that

Then D(r + 2 D) ⩽ ℓ.Since BT (v, ℓ ) is a segment, the index set on the right-hand side of (13.10) has at most 2 ℓ + 1 elements, and every ball of radius D in B has at most 3 · 2D vertices. Theorem 13.14 (Separation from the binary tree) . Let θ satisfy θ1 + θ2 = 1 with 0 < θ 1 < 1,and let T be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ. Then, almost surely, T is not quasi-isometric to the binary tree. Proof. Since 0 < θ 1 < 1, the labels λ(w), w ∈ B, are independent with unbounded support by Lemma 9.2. Thus almost surely sup w λ(w) = ∞, and T contains chains of single-descendant vertices of arbitrary length. Choose a middle vertex vℓ of a chain of length at least 2 ℓ + 2. Its radius-ℓ ball stays inside the chain and is isometric to a segment of Z. Thus Proposition 13.13 shows that almost surely T is not quasi-isometric to B. □

Part 3. Full classification of Galton–Watson trees with finitely supported offspring distributions 

We now extend the classification of Part 2 to all finitely supported offspring laws. We again compare trees by matching labelled pieces and assembling local comparisons into quasi-isometries. The new difficulty is that branching can vary both within each tree and between the two offspring laws. Encoding this variable branching in a binary tree introduces dependence between labels, which requires a matching theorem beyond the independent setting of Part 2. We first prepare the geometric descriptions needed for this encoding, then prove the Markov matching theorem. We combine these ingredients with geometric separation arguments to complete the classification. We then use the classification to prove mutual quasi-isometric embeddability across all supercritical regimes. 14. Matching classes for arbitrary offspring laws 

We first extend the shape construction of §10 to finitely supported offspring laws. For offspring supported on {0, 1, 2}, every split has exactly two surviving children and no dying children. The reduced skeleton is therefore the binary tree, and a split carries no bush. Only the shape law had to be quantised and coupled. With larger offspring numbers, splits may have different arities and may carry bushes of their own. The shape ending at a split can therefore depend on its arity. We construct labels whose law is independent of that arity, while retaining the geometric comparisons needed for quasi-isometry. For two offspring laws, the labels must also have a common law. The arity laws may remain different. We will compare them by replacing each split by a finite binary tree with one leaf for each child. We first describe the general reduced skeleton and develop the shape relabelling in the bushy regime. In the chain regime there are no bushes, and the neck length is already independent of the arity. We recall this simpler label law in §15 before constructing binary encodings for both regimes. Throughout, θ is supercritical with support in {0, . . . , J } and θJ > 0, so that J is its largest supported offspring number. We write f (s) = ∑Jj=0 θj sj for its generating function. We use the probability convention of §2.2: throughout the skeleton and shape constructions, P denotes the survival-conditioned law, together with any independent relabelling variables. 14.1. The reduced skeleton. As in §10.1, a vertex of T (ω) is a skeleton vertex if its subtree is infinite, a split if at least two of its children are skeleton vertices, and a neck vertex if it is a skeleton vertex with exactly one skeleton child. The finite subtrees rooted at the remaining children are the bushes , and a split may carry bushes of its own. Conditioned on T (ω) having infinite diameter, the reduced skeleton is the tree of splits. Its root is the first split at or below the original root. The children of a split in the reduced skeleton are the first splits reached by descending from each of its skeleton children. Starting at the original root or at a skeleton child of a split, we follow the unique skeleton child until we reach a split. We define the neck length m to be the number of vertices visited, including the terminating split. Thus there are m − 1 preceding neck vertices, and m = 1 when the starting vertex is already a split. The descent from the original root defines the initial neck .The decomposition is classical, due to Harris [27] and Sevastyanov [49]. See Athreya–Ney [7, Chapter I.12] and Lyons–Peres [36, Proposition 5.28] for standard accounts. As in Part 2, we rederive its arithmetic to be fairly self-contained and because the chain and shape estimates use the constants explicitly.

Corollary 14.2 (The reduced law has full support) . If θ0 > 0, then ˜νk > 0 for every 2 ⩽ k ⩽ J.Proof. Fix 2 ⩽ k ⩽ J. Among the terms of the coefficient ck of (14.4), the one with j = J

A vertex with J children can have exactly k surviving children for any 2 ⩽ k ⩽ J. This is why no arity in that interval is missing from the reduced law. In the chain regime, q = 0 and every child survives, so the surviving arity is the offspring number itself and gaps may remain in the reduced law. 14.2. Shapes at general arity. For the shape construction and relabelling we assume θ0 > 0. The shape of Definition 10.2 widens in two ways: a neck vertex may now carry several bushes, and the terminating split carries bushes of its own. 

Definition 14.3 (Shapes, general support) . A shape is a pair σ = ( m, (β1, . . . , β m)), where 

m ⩾ 1 and each βi is a finite, possibly empty, list of finite trees with offspring numbers in 

{0, . . . , J }. Its realisation is the finite rooted tree obtained from a path v1 · · · vm by attaching the roots of the trees in βi by an edge at vi. The entry (vertex) is v1, the exit (vertex) is vm,and |σ| is the number of vertices of the realisation. We write S for the set of all shapes. 

Shapes, arities, and assembly. We write S(w) for the shape at a vertex w of the reduced skeleton, k(w) for the arity of the split at w, and m(w) for the neck length of S(w). This neck starts at a skeleton child of the preceding split, or at the original root for the first shape, and ends at w. The lists β1, . . . , β m−1 carry the bushes of the preceding neck vertices, and the exit bouquet βm carries the bushes of the terminating split. A family of shapes indexed by the reduced skeleton is assembled by joining the exit of the shape at w to the entries of the shapes at its children by an edge. The root of the assembly is the entry of the first shape. The space S also contains shapes that have zero probability under the offspring law. We first compute how the bushes at a split depend on its arity.

Conditioning divides the masses by this probability, and one factor 1 − q cancels, which is (14.5). □

Remark. For J ⩾ 3, the joint law is not a product of a law in k and a law in r. Indeed, it gives positive mass to ( J, 0) and (2 , J − 2), by θJ > 0 and q > 0, but none to ( J, J − 2), since 2J − 2 > J . The three points are distinct, whereas a product law giving positive mass to the first two would give positive mass to the third. 14.3. The conditional laws and the relabelling. For the later matching argument, we need labels whose law is independent of the arity. The shape S(w) itself records the exit bouquet, so Lemma 14.4 makes the shape and arity dependent when J ⩾ 3. We use randomised relabelling to ensure independence from the arity. Redistributing the exit bushes among the children creates other dependencies. Sending them to all children makes siblings share a component, while sending them to one designated child makes its law differ from those of its siblings. We instead leave each shape in place and couple its law, conditioned on the terminating arity, to one reference law. We use independent randomness to sample a partner from this coupling and read the label from the partner. The coupling may depend on the arity, but the partner has the same law for every arity. We construct the couplings so that each shape and its partner admit a controlled marked comparison. We first describe the conditional shape laws. Only the exit-bouquet law changes with the terminating arity. 54 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT

Conditional independence. Since distinct shapes use disjoint necks and decorations, the branching property gives the following conditional independence, which we record for later use. 

Lemma 14.7 (Conditional independence) . Conditioned on the arity field (k(w)) w of the reduced skeleton, the shapes S(w) are independent with S(w) ∼ μk(w).

Nets and cuts. The net construction of §10.2 uses only countability, size and marked com-parability, so it applies to the general shape space S. We retain its notation: RD is the set of representatives, rep D the representative map and GD the label graph. The cut used in Lemma 10.8 does not require bounded arity. We record its general form and the additional degree bound available for supported shapes. 

Lemma 14.9 (General-arity dilution) . Let σ ∈ S and let s ⩾ 1 be an integer. There are a finite rooted marked tree T and a surjection P from the realisation of σ onto T , preserving the entry and exit, such that P is a 2s-marked quasi-isometry and 

|T | ⩽ |σ|

s + 1 , diam P −1(v) ⩽ 2s − 2 (v ∈ T ).

If the realisation has at most J children per vertex, then 

|P −1(v)| ⩽ 1 + J(s − 1) (v ∈ T ),

and every vertex of T has at most J(1 + J(s − 1) ) children. For all integers D ⩾ 2 and n ⩾ 1, every family of shapes of size at most n whose distinct members are not D-comparable has at most exp (27( nD −1/3 + 1) ) members. The same bound holds for the members of RD of size at most n.Proof. Apply the greedy cut from the proof of Lemma 10.8. Every part cut off has at least s

vertices, apart from a possible smaller remainder at the root, so there are at most |σ|/s + 1 parts. A remainder passed towards the root has fewer than s vertices. Hence every part has diameter at most 2 s − 2. Root the quotient at the part containing the entry and mark the part containing the exit. For the quotient projection P , the same path estimate gives 

d(P x, P y ) ⩽ d(x, y ) ⩽ (2 s − 1) d(P x, P y ) + 2 s − 2.

Thus P is a 2 s-marked quasi-isometry with the stated fibre bounds. Under the degree bound, a cut part consists of its highest vertex and at most J remainders, each with at most s − 1 vertices. Hence it has at most 1 + J(s − 1) vertices, from which at most 

J(1 + J(s − 1) ) child parts can issue. For the counting bound, the Catalan estimate in Lemma 10.8 applies to rooted plane trees of arbitrary finite arity. For D > 729, set s = ⌊(D/ 72) 1/3⌋. Then 72 s3 ⩽ D and s−1 ⩽ 9D−1/3. If two marked quotient trees are isomorphic, composing their projections and marked quasi-inverses gives a 72 s3-marked quasi-isometry in both directions. The quotient tree has at most n/s + 1 vertices, so the Catalan estimate gives at most exp (3( n/s + 1) ) ⩽ exp (27( nD −1/3 + 1) )

possible marked quotients. For 2 ⩽ D ⩽ 729, the direct bound e3n is no larger than the displayed expression. This proves the assertion for families whose distinct members are not D-comparable and, by the greedy definition of RD, for representatives. □56 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Glued transfer. The glued-transfer argument depends only on the rooted indexing tree and the entry and exit of each copy, not on the number of children. 

Lemma 14.10 (Glued transfer at general arity) . Let (σw)w and (τw)w be families of finite trees with an entry and an exit, indexed by the same rooted tree. Let T and T ′ be their assemblies, formed by joining each parent’s exit to its children’s entries. If K ⩾ 1 and every σw admits a 

K-marked quasi-isometry to τw, then there is a root-preserving 8K2-quasi-isometry T → T ′.Proof. Define the assembly map by a chosen marked quasi-isometry on each copy. The distance formulas Equations (10.1) and (10.2) remain valid for an arbitrary rooted indexing tree, so the termwise estimates in the proof of Lemma 10.9 give the 8 K2 distance bounds. Coarse density holds copy by copy, and the same correction at the root makes the map root-preserving. □

15. Binary encodings of reduced skeletons 

The shape relabelling gives independent labels and arities in the bushy regime. We first recall the corresponding label law in the chain regime. We then encode both kinds of reduced skeleton by the binary tree and show how a matching of the encoded labels gives a quasi-isometry of the original trees. 15.1. Independent necks and arities. We now consider chain-regime laws: θ0 = 0 and 

θ1 ∈ (0 , 1), with finite support and largest supported offspring number J. Every vertex belongs to the skeleton, the extinction probability is q = 0, and the transform of Proposition 14.1 is the identity. There are no bushes, so a shape is determined by its neck length. These lengths lie in 

N, and their level labels from §9.3 lie in N0, with the path graph P of §12.1 as their comparison graph. 

Neck length. For a reduced-skeleton vertex w, let m(w) be the length of the neck ending at w

and let k(w) be the arity of its terminating split. By Proposition 14.1(ii), the pairs (m(w), k (w))

are i.i.d. and have the joint law 

P(m(w) = r, k (w) = j) = θ r−11 θj = θ r−11 (1 − θ1)˜νj (r ⩾ 1, j ⩾ 2) . (15.1) Thus the neck length is geometric with success probability 1 − θ1 and is independent of the terminating arity, which has law ˜ν.

D-scales. For an integer D ⩾ 2, set x(w) = ℓD

> (

m(w)). By (15.1) and Lemma 9.6, its marginal law is 

p(D) 

> i

= θ Di−11 − θ Di+1 −11 (i ⩾ 0) . (15.2) Applying Proposition 14.12, with a shape identified with its neck length and Lk(m, u ) = ℓD(m), shows that the pairs (x(w), k (w)) are i.i.d. with product law Π D = p(D) × ˜ν. Moreover 

p(D)0 = 1 − θ D−11 ⩾ 12 once D is large, and ηP,5/2(p(D)) → 0 as D → ∞ by Lemma 13.1. Thus the chain regime needs no transport relabelling of the kind used in Proposition 14.12. 15.2. The binary encoding. We now return to both regimes. We replace each split by a finite binary tree, keeping its original shape at the root and attaching its children at the leaves. The added vertices have no geometric decoration. If the depths of the replacement trees are bounded, the resulting assembly is quasi-isometric to the original one. This lets us seek a matching on the fixed indexing tree B, even when the original reduced skeletons have different arities. 

Binary profiles. A binary profile is a finite rooted tree in which every nonleaf vertex has two ordered children. For each arity k in a finite set A ⊆ { 2, 3, . . . }, choose a profile Ck with k leaves, listed from left to right. To encode a vertex of arity k, place its original piece at the root of Ck,a one-vertex piece at every other nonleaf vertex, and the encoded child pieces at its leaves in their original order. Every joining edge runs from an exit to an entry. For example, take C3

to be the subtree of B with vertex set {∅ , 1, 2, 21 , 22 } and ordered leaves 1 , 21 , 22. The original piece occupies ∅, the inserted one-vertex piece occupies 2, and the three child pieces attach at the leaves. 

Assemblies. An assembly over a rooted skeleton is the rooted tree obtained from finite tree pieces, each with one entry and one exit, indexed by the vertices of the skeleton. The exit of each piece is joined to the entries of its child pieces, and the entry of the root piece is the root of the assembly. Repeating the construction above gives an assembly indexed by B.

Profiles and distortions. We write 

hC := max  

> k∈A

height( Ck) ⩾ 1.

For an assembly X and its binary encoding XC , let jC : X → XC keep every vertex of the original pieces, and let pC : XC → X fix these vertices and send each inserted vertex to the exit 60 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

of its original piece. Then pC jC = id, and pC is onto and 1-Lipschitz. Each original joining edge becomes a path of length at most hC , while edges inside the pieces are unchanged. Consequently, 

d(x, y ) ⩽ dC

> (

jC (x), j C (y)) ⩽ hC d(x, y ), dC

> (

z, j C pC (z)) ⩽ hC .

Moreover, 

dC (z, z ′) ⩽ 2hC + hC d(pC (z), p C (z′)), d(pC (z), p C (z′)) ⩽ dC (z, z ′).

Both maps fix the root, and these bounds make them ( hC + 1)-quasi-isometries. We record this in the following lemma. 

Lemma 15.1 (Bounded-profile encoding) . For every assembly X over an infinite reduced skeleton whose arities belong to A, the maps jC : X → XC and pC : XC → X are root-preserving 

(hC + 1) -quasi-isometries. They satisfy pC jC = id and

The binary profile process. We next describe the label law separately. Let μ be a probability law on a countable label set with distinguished element 0, and let ν be a probability law on 

A. The corresponding binary profile process is the random label field on B obtained as follows. Draw a label with law μ and, independently, an arity k with law ν. Give the root of Ck this label and its other internal vertices label 0, then attach independent copies of the same process at its k leaves. For an i.i.d. μ × ν label–arity field, the descendant fields below each vertex are independent copies of the original field, so its binary encoding has exactly this law. We record this in the following lemma. 

Lemma 15.2 (Law of the binary encoding) . Suppose that the label–arity pairs at the vertices of an infinite reduced skeleton are i.i.d. with law μ × ν. If every inserted vertex has label 0, then the encoded label field has the law of the binary profile process. 

We will use the preceding metric comparison through a rooted automorphism of B. Such an automorphism may pair an original piece with an inserted one-vertex piece, so the local comparison hypothesis must cover both kinds of pieces. 

Corollary 15.3 (Transfer through binary encodings) . Let XC and YC′ be binary encodings of assemblies X and Y , with all profiles of height at most an integer h ⩾ 1. Suppose that 

K ⩾ 1 and that there is a rooted automorphism π ∈ Aut( B) such that, for every w ∈ B, the piece of XC indexed by w, whether original or inserted, admits a K-marked quasi-isometry to the piece of YC′ indexed by π(w), whether original or inserted. Then there is a root-preserving 

72( h + 1) 2K2-quasi-isometry X → Y .Proof. Reindexing the pieces of YC′ by π preserves its metric and places the compared pieces over the same indexing vertex. Hence Lemma 14.10 gives a root-preserving 8 K2-quasi-isometry 

F : XC → YC′ . By Lemma 15.1, the inclusion jC : X → XC and collapse pC′ : YC′ → Y are (h + 1)-quasi-isometries. The composition bound in Lemma 10.6 therefore makes pC′ F j C aroot-preserving quasi-isometry with constant 3(3( h + 1)8 K2)(h + 1) = 72( h + 1) 2K2. □

Dependence. The encoded labels are generally dependent: an arity choice determines which descendants receive independent labels and which are prescribed to be 0. The next section proves the Markov matching theorem needed for this recursive law. 16. The Markov matching theorem 

We prove Theorem 6.1, first recalling the construction in §6.1. We consider random labellings of the rooted binary tree B with values in the vertex set V of a countable graph G = ( V, E ). Two labels v, w ∈ V are compatible when dG(v, w ) ⩽ 1. We fix a probability law μ on V and a distinguished label 0 ∈ V .We describe the dependence between labels by assigning a type to each vertex of B. Let I be a nonempty countable set of types. Choose Iμ ⊆ I and, for each t ∈ I , a probability law Pt on THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 61 

I × I . Starting from a fixed root type, we generate the labels and child types recursively. At a vertex of type t, we draw the label with law μ if t ∈ I μ, and put 0 otherwise. Independently of this label, we draw the types of its two children with law Pt. Conditioned on these types, the two descendant processes are independent and follow the same rule. The choices at distinct vertices use independent draws. In the binary encoding of §15.2, types in Iμ describe the original vertices, and the remaining types describe the vertices inserted inside the profiles. For two independent realisations started at types s, t , we write Mh(s, t ) for the event that an automorphism of B pairs compatible labels through height h. We write M∞(s, t ) for the event that one automorphism pairs compatible labels at every vertex. The automorphism need not preserve types. We can compare models with different transition laws by taking the disjoint union of their type sets and retaining the transition laws within each model. For a fixed label v ∈ V , the probability of matching a draw from μ is b(v) = μ(BG(v, 1) ). For 

α ⩾ 1, the one-site potentials are 

ηα = ∑

The sum averages over v with law μ, while the additional term in ζα uses the fixed label 0 in place of v. We retain the convention ζα = ∞ when b(0) = 0. When b(0) = 1, no further hypotheses on the types or transitions are needed. When b(0) < 1, we assume that I is finite and impose the conditions of §6.2, which we now recall. We require a partition of I into classes I0, . . . , Ig−1, where g ⩾ 1, such that 

Iμ ⊆ I 0, supp Pt ⊆ I i+1 × I i+1 (t ∈ I i),

with indices read modulo g. A possible type path follows either child in transitions of positive probability. We require the following two conditions: (M1) Positive matching. For every h ⩾ 0 and s, t ∈ I μ, each labelling that occurs with positive probability from s through height h has a positive probability of matching an independent labelling started at t.(M2) Bounded returns. There is an integer H ⩾ 0 such that, whenever s, t belong to the same class Ii, every possible type path from s visits Iμ at some depth n ⩽ H for which a possible type path from t also visits Iμ.The path from t in condition (M2) may depend on the path from s.The contraction estimate at a binary split uses the coefficient from §6.3:

Theorem 6.1 of Part 1, restated. Fix α ⩾ 1 with λα < 1. Assume either that b(0) = 1 , or that the finite-type hypotheses recalled above hold. There are K < ∞ and ε > 0 such that, if ζα ⩽ ε, then for every pair of initial types s, t when

Positive matching probability. To adapt the i.i.d. proof, we fix a finite source labelling produced from one initial type and consider its probability of matching an independent target labelling from another type. This probability can be zero, in which case the potential used in the i.i.d. proof is infinite. We therefore sum only over source labellings with positive matching probability and estimate the probability of the excluded labellings separately. When b(0) = 1, every source labelling of positive probability has a possible match against every target type, so no labellings of positive probability are excluded. 

Zero matching probability. When b(0) < 1, a source labelling of positive probability can have zero probability of matching the target type. We show that one of three events then occurs. The root label is incompatible with 0, or each of the two child subtrees has zero probability of matching some possible child type of the target, or one child subtree has zero probability of matching every such type. If the third event occurs, we apply the same alternatives to that child subtree and all possible child types of the target. Condition (M2) ends this descent along a single source branch. Within H steps, the source type and a type reached by a possible target path both belong to Iμ, where the positivity condition (M1) gives positive matching probability. At each vertex, incompatibility with 0 has probability at most 1 − b(0). Conditioned on the child types, the two child subtrees are independent, so the second event has probability quadratic in the bounds for zero matching probability at smaller heights. When ζα is sufficiently small, these estimates and λα < 1 let us choose a potential bound proportional to ζα that holds at every finite height. Adding the bound for the excluded labellings gives the stated failure estimate. K˝ onig’s lemma and continuity of probability then give the infinite-tree bound. 17. The local matching estimate 

We extend the binary matching estimate of §11.2 to child subtrees with different laws. We first define, for arbitrary source and target laws, the potential restricted to source points with positive matching probability. The estimate for a binary split will also involve the probability of the excluded points and a weighted sum over them, which we estimate separately in §18. We fix the exponent of Theorem 6.1 and abbreviate 

η = ηα(μ), ζ = ζα(μ).

We assume ζ < ∞, so b(0) > 0. 17.1. The restricted potential. Let R be a symmetric relation on a countable set, and say that x matches y when x R y . For a source law ρ and a target law τ on this set, the probability that a fixed source point x matches an independent target point is its matching degree ,

rτ (x) := τ {y : x R y }, qτ (x) := 1 − rτ (x).

We recall the weight from (11.4), writing 

ϕ(q) = ϕα(q) = q

(1 − q)α (0 ⩽ q < 1) .

The restricted potential is 

E(ρ, τ ) := ∑   

> x:rτ(x)>0

> [

1{rτ (X)>0}ϕ(qτ (X))] , X ∼ ρ. THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 63 

We sum over source points with positive matching degree, using their original ρ-mass. Thus this is a restriction of the sum, without conditioning or renormalising the source law. In this part, the subscript of an expectation names the law of the variable averaged over, and for a pair of independent variables it is the product law. The potential is understood to take values in [0 , ∞]. We write E(ρ, τ ; R) when the relation needs to be explicit. If R is reflexive and ρ = τ , every point with positive ρ-mass has positive matching degree, so E(ρ, ρ ; R) equals the i.i.d. potential Φ( R, ρ ) of (11.12). 

Weighting. The denominator in the potential leads us to estimate inverse powers of matching degrees. We use the weight 

Wτ (x) :=

0, rτ (x) = 0 .

Its value at zero matching degree lets us sum over the whole source space while retaining only the points with positive matching degree. 17.2. Four child laws. At a binary split, the source and target each have two child subtrees. Conditioned on their types, the two source subtrees are independent, as are the two target subtrees. Their four laws can all differ, and matching permits either pairing of the children. We now bound probabilities of this choice for arbitrary probability laws. For pairs of points, we use the relation R□ of (11.13): a source pair matches a target pair if either the first points and the second points match, or the first source point matches the second target point and the second source point matches the first target point. The parameter β ∈ [0 , 1] lets us optimise the linear coefficient in the potential bound. We separate the two terms defining λα in (6.2) by writing

by the weighted hypothesis with i = j = 1 for the first factor and by (17.8) for the second. When A2 ∩ { r□ > 0}, the crossed pairing has probability rτ2 (X1)rτ1 (X2) = 0 instead, so

When A3 and A4 hold, the roles of X1 and X2 are exchanged, and the weighted hypotheses with i = 2 give the same bound. Since ϕ(q□) ⩾ 0, the expectation over {r□ > 0} \ { X1, X 2 ∈ F } is at most the sum of the four expectations over Ak ∩ { r□ > 0}, hence at most 4(1 + αM )δ. Adding this to the bound on 

{X1, X 2 ∈ F } proves (17.3). □

18. Labellings with zero matching probability 

We now bound the two error terms in Lemma 17.1 for the laws of the Markov model. We first show that both errors vanish when b(0) = 1. 18.1. Potentials and weights by type. For t ∈ I and h ⩾ 0, let ρt,h be the law of the labels through height h for a process started at type t, with the type information discarded. Recall that x ≈h y means that an automorphism matches the two height-h labellings x, y . We use the notation of §17.1 for these laws and the relation ≈h, indexed by type. Thus the matching degree and the weight of a source labelling x against a target of type t are 

rt,h (x) := rρt,h (x) = ρt,h {y : x ≈h y}, Wt,h (x) := Wρt,h (x),

and the restricted potential of a source type s against a target type t is

We prove by induction on height that every source labelling of positive probability has positive matching degree against every target type. At height zero, each label in supp μ matches itself and is compatible with 0, while 0 is compatible with every label in the support. This covers both choices of root label law on each side. For the induction step, fix a source labelling of positive probability. Some source transition of positive probability produces its two child labellings with positive probability, since the set of transitions is countable. Choose any target transition of positive probability. By induction, the first source child has positive matching degree against the first target child type, as does 68 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. the second against the second. Conditioned on the target transition, independence of the target root and children makes the full matching degree positive. Consequently, for all s, t and h ⩾ 0, 

ρs,h {rt,h = 0 } = 0 . (18.2) Both error terms in Lemma 17.1 therefore vanish, including when the set of types is infinite. For the remainder of this section, we assume the finite-type hypotheses of Theorem 6.1 and bound the two error terms when b(0) may be less than one. An impossible comparison can pass from a source labelling to one of its children. ϕ(1 − b(0)) + (αϕ (1 − b(0)) + b(0) 1−α)Q(M ).

If the target root has prescribed label 0, fix a source root label v ∈ supp μ ∪ { 0}. If v / ∈ BG(0 , 1), the root labels are incompatible, so every corresponding full matching degree is zero and its term is omitted from the restricted potential. If v ∈ BG(0 , 1), the root labels are compatible and the full matching degree equals the child-pair degree. The conditional potential is therefore at most Q(M ). Averaging over a random source root label, or retaining the prescribed source label 0, does not increase this bound. By the definition of Dμ, the constant terms are at most ζ and the three coefficients of Q(M )are at most Dμ. Thus, for every pair of initial types under consideration, the full potential satisfies 

Eh+1 (s, t ) ⩽ ζ + DμQ(M ).

To bound Dμ in terms of ζ, convexity gives x − xα ⩽ (α − 1)(1 − x) for 0 < x ⩽ 1. Dividing by xα yields 

x1−α ⩽ 1 + ( α − 1) ϕ(1 − x).

We apply this with x = b(v) and average over μ, or take x = b(0) for a prescribed source label. In both cases the averaged potential is at most ζ, so 

Dμ ⩽ 1 + (2 α − 1) ζ < ∞. □

Consequently, the bound M is preserved from one height to the next provided 

ζ + DμQ(M ) ⩽ M. (19.3) Because Q(M ) ⩾ 0, it is enough to impose the following scalar condition, expressed in terms of the one-site potential ζ and the child-error bounds Z and E(M ) entering Q(M ): 

ζ + (1 + (2 α − 1) ζ)Q(M ) ⩽ M.

20. Product laws with a common branching semigroup 

We now apply Theorem 6.1 to two product laws μ × νL and μ × νR with the same label law 

μ, where the arity laws νσ have nonempty finite supports 

Sσ := supp νσ ⊆ { 2, 3, . . . } (σ ∈ { L, R }).

We further assume that both measures generate the same branching semigroup: Λ := Λ νL = ⟨k − 1 : k ∈ SL⟩ = ⟨k − 1 : k ∈ SR⟩ = Λ νR .

As before, the exponent satisfies α ⩾ 1 and λα < 1. The resulting uniform estimate is recorded in Proposition 20.6; it supplies the matching input in the proofs of Propositions 22.5 and 22.7. THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 77 

Shared arities. We recall the encoding of §15.2. For each supported arity k, we choose a binary profile Ck with k leaves. On side σ, the encoded field has the recursive law of Lemma 15.2, with label law μ, arity law νσ and distinguished label 0. The two encodings will use a nonempty set of shared arities satisfying 

Score ⊆ SL ∩ SR, Λ = ⟨k − 1 : k ∈ Score ⟩,

which we call the common generating core , or simply the common core . We choose the same profile Ck on both sides for each k ∈ Score . Every other profile is obtained by composing core profiles. We continue to write Ck on either side, allowing the noncore profiles to differ between sides. We fix these finite lists of profiles before varying the arity probabilities or the label law. 

Replacements. Replacing a leaf of an a-leaf profile by a b-leaf profile gives a + b − 1 leaves. Thus each replacement adds the shifted arity b − 1. We first use this identity to construct the profiles from common semigroup generators, then verify the Markov hypotheses. 20.1. The common generators. An atom of Λ is a positive element which cannot be written as the sum of two positive elements of Λ. We write A for the set of atoms and note that it is nonempty: the least positive element of Λ cannot be a sum of two smaller positive elements. We write s(Λ) for this least positive element and put 

s0 := min A = s(Λ) = min(Λ \ { 0}).

We start with a simple combinatorial observation. 

Lemma 20.1. The set A is finite, generates Λ, and satisfies 

{a + 1 : a ∈ A} ⊆ SL ∩ SR.

For every k ⩾ 2 with k − 1 ∈ Λ, any expression of k − 1 as a sum of atoms has at most 

⌊(k − 1) /s 0⌋ summands. Proof. Express an atom as a sum of generators from either shifted support. There must be exactly one summand, since two or more would decompose the atom into two positive elements of Λ. Thus every atom belongs to both shifted supports, which also proves finiteness. We prove generation by induction on positive elements of Λ. An element which is not an atom is the sum of two smaller positive elements, each a sum of atoms by induction. Finally, each atomic summand is at least s0, which proves the bound on their number. □

For the main uniform statement below, we take Score := {a + 1 : a ∈ A} as the canonical common generating core and choose an ( a + 1)-leaf profile Ca+1 for each atom a. For example, the nonnested supports SL = {4, 6, 7} and SR = {4, 6, 9} have common shifted semigroup ⟨3, 5⟩

and atomic core {4, 6}, so the uniform bounds below require the masses of arities 4 and 6, but not those of 7 and 9, to remain bounded away from zero. For every supported arity k, choose an expression 

k − 1 = a1 + · · · + ank , ai ∈ A .

When k = a + 1 is a core arity we use the one-term expression. Starting from a single leaf, replace a current leaf by Ca1+1 , then a current leaf by Ca2+1 , and continue. The resulting profile 

Ck has 1 + ∑ 

> i

ai = k leaves. We can fix all choices using only the supports. After choosing one core profile for each atom, order the atoms, choose a shortest expression, order its summands, and at each step replace a shallowest leaf, resolving ties lexicographically. We make the same choices for arities present on both sides. The resulting profiles are independent of the arity masses and the label law. For a chain-regime offspring law θ, the reduced law ˜ν is the original law conditioned on arities 

k ⩾ 2. Its shifted support differs from that of θ only by the element 0, and hence Λ˜ν = Λ θ.

Thus chain laws with the same branching semigroup give reduced arity laws to which the preceding construction applies. For a bushy-regime law, Corollary 14.2 gives ˜ν2 > 0. Hence two bushy laws have the common core arity 2, and repeated replacement by the two-leaf profile constructs every required binary profile. 78 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

20.2. The depth of the profiles. The depth determines the return bound H used in the theorem. We record a direct bound. 

Lemma 20.2. If every core profile Ca+1 has height at most L and every replacement uses a shallowest current leaf, then 

height( Ck) ⩽ L + ⌊log 2(k − s0)⌋.

Proof. We first note that a full binary tree with n leaves has a leaf of depth at most ⌊log 2 n⌋.Writing |v| for the depth of a vertex, we have  

> ∑
> va leaf

2−| v| = 1 .

This follows by induction, since the two branches each contribute one half of their corresponding sum. If all leaves had greater depth, the displayed sum would be at most n2−⌊ log 2 n⌋− 1 < 1. Just before a replacement there are at most k − s0 leaves, since that replacement adds at least s0

leaves. The new leaves have depth at most L larger than the chosen leaf. Every final leaf was created in one of the replacements, proving the upper bound. □

Returns. For the simplest return argument, choose Ca+1 with one root child a leaf and the other a balanced full binary tree with a leaves. For a = 1, both children are leaves. These profiles have a leaf of depth one and height at most 

L := 1 + ⌈log 2 max A⌉ .

The lemma gives

20.3. Verification of the Markov hypotheses. We now verify the hypotheses of Theorem 6.1 for any finite choice of profiles built from a common generating core as described at the start of this section. We use hC for the maximum profile height over both sides. 20.3.1. Types and transitions. On side σ, an original vertex has the type Fσ. For every profile 

Ck on side σ and every internal vertex w of Ck other than its root, we introduce a type recording the subtree of Ck rooted at w. When two such subtrees are identical, we may use one type for both. The type set I consists of FL, FR and these subtree types. The root label of Fσ has law 

μ, and the root label of every subtree type is 0, so 

Iμ = {FL, F R}.

The transitions are as follows. With probability νσ(k), the type Fσ gives its two children the types of the two children of the root of Ck, where a child that is a leaf of Ck receives the type 

Fσ. A subtree type gives its two children the types determined in the same way by the subtree it records, with probability one. Conditioned on the chosen child types, the two child labellings are independent, so these root label laws and transitions define a process of the form in §6.1, in which the original vertices of the encoding are the vertices of type FL or FR.Counting both sides, we have #I ⩽ 2 + ∑

20.3.2. Positive matching probability. We assume b(0) > 0, which holds when ζα < ∞. Every realised label belongs to supp μ ∪ { 0}, and each label in this set has a compatible label of positive 

μ-mass. Indeed, 

b(v) ⩾ μ(v) > 0 (v ∈ supp μ), b(0) > 0.

We use this fact to replace a composite target profile by successive draws of its core profiles without losing the possibility of a match. We use the following core-replacement construction twice. Prescribe the values of the label and arity draws at finitely many original vertices, with every prescribed value in the support of its law, and suppose that these draws determine an encoded labelling through a given height. Fix a matching with a source labelling whose labels belong to supp μ ∪ { 0}. For any chosen occurrence of Ck, replace the prescribed arity k at its root by the root core arity in the fixed composition defining Ck. At each additional core-profile root, prescribe its core arity and a label of positive μ-mass compatible with the source label paired to that vertex. Retain the label at the original root, all other prescribed draws and the fixed matching. The binary profile is unchanged. Every value in the new finite assignment has positive probability, so independence implies that the event on which all these values are drawn has positive probability on the original side. If every profile occurrence is replaced, all prescribed arities are core arities, and the same event has positive probability on the other side as well. 

Lemma 20.3. For every h ⩾ 0 and s, t ∈ { FL, F R}, each labelling of positive probability under 

ρs,h has positive matching degree against ρt,h .Proof. Fix s = Fσ and a labelling x through height h with ρs,h {x} > 0. Under ρs,h the labelling is a function of the labels and arities drawn at the original vertices of depth at most h. The countably many assignments of values to these draws partition the event that the resulting labelling is x, so one assignment has positive probability. Apply the core-replacement construction to every profile occurrence represented in this assignment, use the same core profiles on the side of t, and retain the source label at every original root. At each additional core-profile root, the source label is 0, so we prescribe a positive-mass label compatible with 0. With the identity matching, the resulting finite assignment determines a labelling y on the side of t such that 

ρt,h {y} > 0 and x matches y. Hence rt,h (x) ⩾ ρt,h {y} > 0. □

The same construction lets us use only core arities in the inverse-mixture bound of (18.5). Fix a side σ and a finite child height h. Let Y1, Y 2 be the two target child labellings through height h. When a path reaches a profile leaf, it reaches the next original vertex and hence a type in Iμ. The possible distances for core profiles form the set 

Rcore := {| v| : v is a leaf of Ck, k ∈ Score }.

Here |v| denotes depth from the profile root. We put 

g := gcd Rcore , Γ := ⟨r : r ∈ Rcore ⟩.

Concatenating paths through core profiles realises every additional length in Γ. Every leaf depth of a composite profile is also such a sum, and hence a multiple of g.We use these congruences to assign the type classes directly. Put FL, F R in I0. For an inserted type t, let d be the depth of any leaf in its remaining finite profile, and assign its class by 

t ∈ I i ⇐⇒ i + d ≡ 0 (mod g) (0 ⩽ i < g ).

This does not depend on the chosen leaf. Indeed, the remaining depths are obtained by subtracting the depth of the inserted vertex from full-profile leaf depths, all multiples of g. It also does not depend on which occurrence of an identified subtree is used. Descending to an inserted child decreases each remaining depth by one. If the child is original, its remaining distance was one, so its parent’s class is Ig−1. At an original vertex, the same congruence applied to each root branch gives child class I1. Thus every transition advances both child types by one class, with indices read modulo g, as required in §6.2. 

Lemma 20.5. If some core profile has a leaf at depth one, then g = 1 and one may take H = 2 hC .Proof. The generators r/g , for r ∈ Rcore , have greatest common divisor one. Hence the numerical semigroup that they generate contains every sufficiently large integer; see Niven–Zuckerman– Montgomery [44, Section 1.2]. t ∈ I μ. Appending paths through core profiles extends it to possible target paths ending in Iμ at every depth in {d + m : m ∈ Γ}. If n ⩾ d + cret and n ≡ d (mod g), then n − d is a multiple of g

at least cret , hence lies in Γ. Thus a possible target path of length n ends in Iμ for every such n.On the source side, every possible path reaches Iμ within hC steps, and the gaps between successive visits are at most hC . The first visit of this path to Iμ at or after depth hC + cret 

therefore occurs at a depth k with

An obstruction for preassigned profiles. The common-profile requirement cannot be omitted: equal branching semigroups alone do not ensure matching for arbitrary fixed binary encodings. The following counterexample works for every fixed potential exponent. Take 

νL = δ4, νR = 12 δ4 + 12 δ7,

and use balanced binary profiles at both arities. Both shifted supports generate 3 N0. The four-leaf profile has all leaves at depth two, whereas the seven-leaf profile has leaves at depths two and three. In the left process every odd-depth vertex therefore has prescribed label 0. Use the path relation 0–1–2, including reflexive pairs, and put 

μ(0) = 12 , μ(1) = 12 − t, μ(2) = t, 0 < t < 12 .

Then 

ηα(μ) = t

2(1 − t)α + 2 α−1t −→ 0 (t ↓ 0) .

Thus the one-site hypothesis can be made arbitrarily strong. In the right process follow original vertices u0, u 1, . . . , choosing a fixed leaf at depth two when the current arity is four and a fixed leaf at depth three when it is seven. Given the process up to ui before its new arity is drawn, exactly one of the two equiprobable arities makes |ui+1 | odd. 82 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

The label at ui+1 equals 2 with independent probability t. Successive conditioning therefore gives

Every rooted automorphism preserves depth. A right label 2 at odd depth cannot match any left label at that depth, since all of them are 0. Also |ui| ⩽ 3i. Hence 

P(M3n) ⩽ (1 − t/ 2) n −→ 0, P(M∞) = 0 .

The common-profile construction avoids this obstruction. Here the only atom is 3. Choose a four-leaf profile C4 and build C7 by replacing a leaf of C4 by another copy of C4. We may retain the balanced C4, in which case every return to an original vertex has even depth and we may use g = 2. Alternatively, use a four-leaf profile with a depth-one leaf. Both choices give a common presentation and the matching conclusion. 21. Evaluation of the bounds 

The proof of Theorem 6.1 reduces the quantitative estimate to a scalar barrier inequality for

21.3. The exponent range. We show that λα is continuous and strictly decreasing on [1 , ∞), so that the exponents with λα < 1 form an open half-line. For continuity, let 0 < s ⩽ 1 and 1 ⩽ α ⩽ α′. Then 0 ⩽ sα − sα′

A lower bound for the linear coefficient. We show that the linear coefficient of any uniform four-law contraction for the potential ϕ(q) = q/ (1 − q)α is bounded below. The same inequality holds with M∞ in place of Mh. Thus the linear order O(ηα) cannot be improved to o(ηα) uniformly. 22. Proof of the complete classification 

We first handle the full tree class, extend the geometric obstructions of §13.5 to general finite supports, and separate chain laws with different branching semigroups. We then prove universality in the bushy and chain regimes. The relabellings and binary encodings constructed in Sections 14 and 15 allow us to apply the Markov matching theorem. Matching the encoded labels gives a quasi-isometry of the original trees. 22.1. Full trees and regime obstructions. We first prove quasi-isometry to the binary tree in the full tree regime. We call a rooted tree full if every vertex has at least two children. In the geometric group theory literature a tree is called bushy if every point lies within uniformly bounded distance of a vertex with at least three unbounded complementary components. Full trees of bounded valence are bushy in this geometric sense. Throughout the classification, however, the bushy regime means regime (B), whose realisations also contain finite bushes. 

Proposition 22.1 (Full trees; Mosher–Sageev–Whyte [43, §2.1]) . Let N ∈ N with N ⩾ 2,and let T ⊆ N (N ) be a rooted subtree such that v1, v 2 ∈ T for every v ∈ T . Then there is a root-preserving quasi-isometry T → B.90 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Proof. The tree T has bounded valence. Every nonroot vertex separates at least three unbounded complementary components, and the root lies at distance one from such a vertex, so T is bushy. The cited result gives a quasi-isometry f : T → B. Replacing f (∅) by the root of B changes f by a bounded amount, so the resulting map remains a quasi-isometry and preserves the root. □

We use the terminology of §13.5: rays, lines and bushes of depth h at a vertex. 

Lemma 22.2 (General-support regime obstructions) . Let θ be a finitely supported supercritical offspring distribution, and let T be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ, conditioned on having infinite diameter. (i) Almost surely T contains three rays that pairwise meet only in their common initial vertex. (ii) If θ0 = 0 , then almost surely there is a finite r such that every vertex of T lies within distance r of some line in T .(iii) If θ0 > 0, then almost surely T has bushes of unbounded depth. (iv) If θ1 > 0, then almost surely, for every ℓ ∈ N, there is a vertex vℓ whose ball BT (vℓ, ℓ ) is isometric to a segment of Z.Proof. Write T ∗ for T when θ0 = 0 and for its skeleton when θ0 > 0. By Proposition 14.1(i), the tree T ∗ never dies out and is a subtree of T carrying the induced metric. The proofs of Lemma 13.12(i) and (ii) use only these facts and that every neck ends almost surely at a split. The latter follows from Proposition 14.1(ii). Those proofs therefore apply unchanged; at a split of arity greater than two, we simply select two children. This proves Items (i) and (ii). For Item (iii), let q ∈ (0 , 1) be the extinction probability and set J = max supp θ. At a unary skeleton vertex, the event that the total offspring number is J has positive conditional weight 

Jθ J (1 − q)q J−1. On this event there is a bush with offspring law θ†. Since θ† 

> 0

= θ0/q > 0and θ† 

> J

= θJ q J−1 > 0, this bush has positive probability of containing the complete J-ary tree of any prescribed height. Moreover, ˜θ1 = f ′(q) > 0, so the conditional independence in Proposition 14.1(ii) and (iii) allows the Borel–Cantelli argument from Lemma 13.12(iii) to apply. Finally, suppose that θ1 > 0, and let q be the extinction probability. At a unary skeleton vertex, the conditional probability that the total offspring number is one equals θ1/f ′(q) > 0. Thus, for every L, a given neck has positive probability of containing L consecutive vertices with exactly one child. Conditioned on the infinite reduced skeleton, these events are independent by Proposition 14.1. The second Borel–Cantelli lemma therefore gives such chains of arbitrary length almost surely. The midpoint vℓ of a chain of length at least 2 ℓ + 2 has BT (vℓ, ℓ ) contained in that chain and isometric to a segment of Z, proving Item (iv). □

Coarse branching counts. The branching semigroup also separates distinct chain classes. We now prove that the coarse branching counts recorded by Definition 1.1 are quasi-isometry invariants of chain-regime realisations. 

Proposition 22.3 (Separation within the chain regime) . Let θ and θ′ be finitely supported supercritical offspring distributions in the chain regime with Λθ̸ = Λ θ′ , and let T , T ′ be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions. Then almost surely T̸ ≃ T ′.

Obstructions. Before proving Proposition 22.3, we establish a deterministic obstruction lemma. Recall that we write χx for the number of children of a vertex x. We extend the notion of a 

branching semigroup to rooted locally finite trees S without leaves by setting Λ( S) := ⟨χx − 1 : x ∈ S⟩ .

For the Galton–Watson trees considered below, this agrees almost surely with the branching semigroup of the offspring law. For L ∈ N and n ⩾ 3, a vertex v of a tree T is the centre of an n-star of length L if BT (v, L ) is obtained by identifying one endpoint of n disjoint paths of length L.

Lemma 22.4 (Deterministic star obstruction) . Let S be a rooted locally finite tree without leaves, let n ⩾ 3 satisfy n − 2 /∈ Λ( S), and let D ∈ N with D ⩾ 1. There are L, L ′ ∈ N such that, THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 91

Proof of Proposition 22.3. Exchanging the two laws if necessary, we may assume that Λ θ ⊈ Λθ′ .Hence there is some k ⩾ 2 such that θk > 0 and k − 1 /∈ Λθ′ . For each L, consider sufficiently separated vertices 1d. The event that 1 d has k children and that the L vertices along its parent arm and each child arm are unary has a fixed positive probability, and these events are independent. The second Borel–Cantelli lemma therefore gives infinitely many n-stars of length L almost surely. Intersecting over L ∈ N, we obtain such stars with arbitrarily distant centres. Fix D ⩾ 1. A pair with the lengths and separation given by Lemma 22.4 for S = T ′ rules out a D-quasi-isometry T → T ′. Taking the countable intersection over D ∈ N proves the proposition. □

22.2. Universality in the bushy and chain regimes. We now compare two trees in the same regime. The relabelling constructions give a common label law whose potential tends to zero as the geometric scale D increases. We choose the binary profiles independently of D, apply Theorem 6.1 at the admissible exponent 5 /2, using λ5/2 < 1 from §6.3, and then glue the marked comparisons between matched pieces. Since the profiles and arity laws are fixed, the matching constants are independent of D.

Neck lengths and one-vertex pieces. For chain laws, Lemma 9.7 already gives a common law for the quantised neck lengths. We also need to compare a neck with an inserted one-vertex piece when their labels are compatible. The following observation includes this case and applies to any two chain laws, independently of their branching semigroups. 

Lemma 22.6 (Comparison of labelled necks) . Let θ and θ′ be chain-regime offspring laws. Label a neck of the first law by ℓD and one of the second law by the randomised map of Lemma 9.7, at any integer scale D for which that lemma applies. The cut-point bounds in Lemma 9.7 place the length of a neck in class j between c−Dj and

If m, m ′ > 1, round the affine map between the endpoints of the two paths. The ratio of their numbers of edges differs from m/m ′ by at most a factor of 2, and rounding changes a distance by at most 2. The maps in both directions are therefore marked quasi-isometries with constant 4( c+/c −)D2. If either path has one vertex, the other has at most ( c+/c −)D2 vertices, and the constant maps have the same bound. □

Proposition 22.7 (Universality within a chain class) . Use independent uniform variables at the vertices, independent also of the two trees. By (15.1), the geometric neck lengths are independent of the arities. 22.3. Completion of the classification. Recall that the possible branching semigroups are the additive submonoids of N0 generated by a nonempty finite set of positive integers, and that (C Λ) denotes the chain class with branching semigroup Λ. 

Theorem 1.2 of Part 1, restated. All finite Galton–Watson realisations form the quasi-isometry class (Fin) , and no finite tree is quasi-isometric to an infinite tree. Let θ and θ′ be finitely supported offspring distributions, each either supercritical or with 

θ1 = 1 , and let T , T ′ be independent Galton–Watson trees with these offspring distributions, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Every such distribution belongs to exactly one of the following classes: (R) θ1 = 1 , the ray class, in which T is the ray. (F) θ0 = θ1 = 0 , the full tree class, in which T admits a quasi-isometry to the binary tree. 

(C Λ) θ0 = 0 < θ 1 < 1 and Λθ = Λ , the chain class indexed by the branching semigroups Λ.(B) θ0 > 0, the bushy class. Two independent realisations belonging to the same class almost surely admit a root-preserving quasi-isometry. If they belong to different classes, then they are almost surely not quasi-isometric. Proof of Theorem 1.2. All finite metric spaces are quasi-isometric. A quasi-isometry from a bounded space has bounded image, and coarse density then forces its target to be bounded. This proves the assertions about (Fin). For finite trees, the constant map to the target root is root-preserving. Suppose first that θ and θ′ belong to the same class. In class (R), the two trees are the ray. In class (F), Proposition 22.1 gives root-preserving quasi-isometries from both trees to the binary tree, and hence a root-preserving quasi-isometry between them. In a class (C Λ), Proposition 22.7 gives such a map almost surely, and Proposition 22.5 does so in class (B). Suppose next that the two laws belong to different classes. If they belong to (C Λ) and (C Λ′ )with Λ ̸ = Λ ′, then Proposition 22.3 gives T̸ ≃ T ′ almost surely. It remains to consider laws in different coarse regimes. 94 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

Suppose one law belongs to class (R). Its realisation is the ray, while the other law is supercritical. By Lemma 22.2(i), the other realisation almost surely contains three rays that pairwise meet only in their common initial vertex. It follows from Proposition 13.11 that T̸ ≃ T ′

almost surely. Suppose one law belongs to class (F) and the other to a chain class. The chain realisation almost surely satisfies the thin-ball hypothesis of Proposition 13.13, by Lemma 22.2(iv). It is therefore not quasi-isometric to B, whereas the realisation in the full tree class is quasi-isometric to B by Proposition 22.1. Transitivity of quasi-isometry gives T̸ ≃ T ′ almost surely. In the remaining cases, one law belongs to class (B) and the other to class (F) or a chain class. After exchanging their names, we may assume that θ0 > 0 = θ′

> 0

. By Lemma 22.2(ii) and (iii), the realisation T almost surely has bushes of unbounded depth, while there is a finite r such that every vertex of T ′ lies within distance r of a line. Hence Proposition 13.10 gives T̸ ≃ T ′

almost surely.
