23. Quasi-isometric embeddings 

We now use the classification to compare trees by quasi-isometric embeddings. We recall the theorem from the introduction. 

Theorem 1.3 of Part 1, restated. Let θ be a finitely supported supercritical offspring distribution, and let T be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution θ, conditioned on having infinite diameter. Almost surely, there are quasi-isometric embeddings 

T ↪→ B and B ↪→ T .

The embedding into B follows from the bounded offspring support. For the reverse direction, the property B ≼ X is preserved under quasi-isometry of X, by composition. We may therefore choose a convenient offspring law within each chain class. For a bushy realisation, it suffices to embed into its survival skeleton, since the inclusion of the skeleton with its original unit edges is isometric. We first prove the pruning lemma that supplies a binary subtree for the chosen representatives, then complete the proof of Theorem 1.3. 23.1. Pruning a concentrated offspring law. Fix an integer J ⩾ 24 and put m = ⌊J/ 2⌋ + 1. We use the independent offspring variables ( χv)v∈N (J) on the ambient word tree, including at words outside the Galton–Watson realisation. Define the sets of vertices surviving successive pruning rounds by 

V0 = N (J),Vn+1 = {v ∈ N (J) : χv = J, #{j ∈ { 1, . . . , J } : vj ∈ Vn} ⩾ m} ,V∞ = ⋂

23.2. The embeddings and strict hierarchy. 

Proof of Theorem 1.3. Choose N ⩾ 2 bounding the offspring support. The word representation gives an isometric inclusion T ↪→ N (N ), and Proposition 22.1 gives N (N ) ≃ B. Composing these maps proves T ≼ B.THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 97 

For the reverse direction, the full tree case follows directly from Proposition 22.1. Next suppose that T belongs to a chain class (C Λ), and let A be the finite set of positive atoms of Λ. These atoms generate Λ, by Lemma 20.1. Since Λ contains arbitrarily large positive multiples of any one of its atoms, we may choose an integer J such that 

J ⩾ 24 , J > 1 + max A, J − 1 ∈ Λ.

Choose 0 < ε ⩽ 1/8 and a probability law ν giving positive mass to each element of {1} ∪ { a +1 : a ∈ A} and no mass elsewhere. Define a representative offspring law by

= 1 − ε, θ′

with zero mass above J. Its branching semigroup is Λθ′ = ⟨A ∪ { J − 1}⟩ = Λ ,

< 1, so it belongs to the chain class (C Λ). In particular, only offspring numbers whose excess lies in Λ receive mass. Since θ′

⩾ 7/8, Lemma 23.1 shows that its realisation T ′

almost surely contains an isometric copy of B.Take T ′ independently of T . By Theorem 1.2, the two realisations are almost surely quasi-isometric. On the joint probability-one event where both assertions hold, we compose the binary-tree inclusion with a quasi-isometry T ′ → T . Thus almost every T admits a quasi-isometric embedding of B. Since Λ was arbitrary, this covers all chain classes. Finally, suppose that T is in the bushy regime and let T ∗ be its survival skeleton with the original unit edges. By Proposition 14.1 and Corollary 14.2, the skeleton has a finitely supported supercritical offspring law ˜θ with ˜θ0 = 0, 0 < ˜θ1 < 1 and ˜θ2 > 0. Its branching semigroup is therefore N0, so the chain case gives B ≼ T ∗ almost surely. Composing with the isometric inclusion T ∗ ↪→ T gives B ≼ T . □

For any fixed pair of finitely supported supercritical laws and independent survival-conditioned realisations, Theorem 1.3 gives, almost surely, 

T ≼ B ≼ T ′, T ′ ≼ B ≼ T .

We finish by checking the strict comparisons with the finite and ray categories. Every finite tree 

F is quasi-isometric to a point, which embeds isometrically into the ray N0. The lower metric bound prevents an unbounded space from embedding into a bounded space, so F ≺ N0.Every infinite locally finite rooted tree contains an infinite ray from its root, whose inclusion is isometric. Thus N0 ≼ T . By Lemma 22.2(i), a supercritical realisation conditioned on survival almost surely contains three rays meeting only at their common initial vertex. The proof of Proposition 13.11, including its use of Lemma 13.9, uses only the two metric inequalities and does not use coarse surjectivity. The same argument therefore excludes a quasi-isometric embedding into N0. Consequently, almost surely, 

F ≺ N0 ≺ T .

These comparisons give the strict hierarchy stated in the introduction. The distinct quasi-isometry classes within its supercritical category remain those of Theorem 1.2. 98 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT

Appendix B. The Lean formalisations 

B.1. Reproduction and scope. The definitive formalisation will be distributed as an im-mutable Palomar version. The version will contain the source, its dependency lock file and the audit configuration. The development uses Lean and Mathlib version v4.32.2 . From the root of that version, the command 

lake build 

builds the four libraries. The command 

python3 -B scripts/check_paper_correspondence.py 

checks that every labelled paper declaration is represented in the correspondence manifest, that every listed Lean name occurs in the source and that the thirteen headline names agree across the audit files. This is a static consistency check and does not replace the build. The command 

./comparator-audit.sh THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 105 

additionally checks the thirteen headline declarations in Challenge.lean against their proofs in Solution.lean . A successful run of the static checker prints OK . A successful build and comparator audit terminate without errors. The comparator reports that the selected declarations agree and that their dependency closures use only propext , Classical.choice and Quot.sound .The current formalised scope consists of the thirteen audited endpoints in the first table and the remaining paper declarations in the correspondence tables, including Theorem 1.3, Lemma 23.1 together with the assertions of its proof, and the strict hierarchy of §23. The intentional exclusions are recorded at the end of Appendix B.3. Thus the build checks the Lean declarations, while the paper-to-Lean correspondence recorded here remains a mathematical assertion to be checked by the reader. B.2. The audited statement surface. Lean’s kernel is the small proof checker that verifies the type of every declaration. The dependency closure of a theorem consists of all declarations used, directly or indirectly, in its proof. The term sorry is a placeholder that makes Lean accept a statement without a proof. The file Challenge.lean , which imports Mathlib alone, is the project-specific human-audited statement surface. It contains the minimal vocabulary needed for the headline results and one 

sorry for each of the thirteen declarations in the following table. The corresponding declarations in Solution.lean have the same types and supply proofs from the four libraries. The comparator checks the equality of these types, traverses the dependency closure of each solution theorem and rejects any unpermitted axiom or sorry . It does not decide whether the formal definitions express the intended mathematics. That last comparison, between Challenge.lean and the paper, is the human part of the audit. Lean’s kernel, Mathlib, the pinned toolchain and the comparator implementation remain part of the technical trusted base.              

> Paper statement Declaration in Challenge.lean
> Theorem 5.1, leaf bound Challenge.audit graph leaf matching bound
> Theorem 5.1, full bound Challenge.audit graph full matching bound
> Theorem 5.1, infinite tree Challenge.audit exists infinite tree matching graphAut
> Theorem 1.2, finite class: bounded graphs Challenge.audit bounded graph qi point
> Theorem 1.2, finite class: extinction Challenge.audit extinction of not supercritical
> Theorem 1.2, complete classification Challenge.audit full classification ae iff
> Theorem 1.3, mutual embeddability Challenge.audit mutual embeddability
> Theorem 1.4, almost-sure statement Challenge.audit twovalue ae tree family
> Theorem 1.4, quantitative rate Challenge.audit twovalue rate tree
> Theorem 6.1, finite alternative Challenge.audit markov matching finite
> Theorem 6.1, zero-compatible alternative Challenge.audit markov matching zero
> Theorem 6.1, finite alternative at infinite height
> Challenge.audit markov matching finite infinite
> Theorem 6.1, zero-compatible alternative at infinite height
> Challenge.audit markov matching zero infinite

B.3. Deviations from the text. The formal statements agree with the printed ones except for the differences below. In each item we first recall the formulation in the paper and then describe its Lean counterpart. 

• The paper uses the exact values L5/2 = 6 √15 /125, K5/2 = 27 ( 57 )5/2 and c5/2 = 8 √2 − 2in the i.i.d. pointwise estimates. Lean checks the same estimates with the respective rational upper bounds 14 /75, 1 /8 and 28 /3. 

• The infinite-height conclusion of Theorem 5.1 is stated for two given independent labellings by vertices of a simple graph, where compatibility means equality or adjacency. In 

Challenge.lean , the graph is replaced by an arbitrary reflexive symmetric relation, and the theorem instead asserts the existence of a probability space carrying two independent consistent labelling processes. The library constructs that space from the infinite product measure. 

• The paper assumes that the offspring law is supported on {0, . . . , J } and that θJ > 0, so J is the largest supported arity. Lean stores the law in an ambient arity N together with a bound J ⩽ N , without assuming positive mass at J, and internally replaces a nonminimal bound by the actual largest supported arity. 106 J. S. ATHREYA AND S. TROSCHEIT 

• In the paper, the Harris decomposition describes each bush by its full offspring field, with the fields conditionally independent and distributed according to the conjugate law. Lean states the conditional law after mapping each field to its realised tree. This omits the unused field coordinates outside that tree, which remain unconditioned. 

• The paper incorporates the four largeness conditions in (13.2) into the thresholds 

D0, D 1 appearing in Theorem 1.4 and Proposition 13.3. Lean first proves rate estimates under the four explicit conditions and then uses ChainClasses.forall scale to ob-tain a suitable scale. In that namespace, the wrappers exists twovalue rate tree 

and exists crosslaw rate tree recover the printed threshold statements. The fi-nal shape and classification declarations bushy ae tree , bushy ae shape tree two law ,

bushy general ae and chain general ae likewise have no additional largeness hypoth-esis. 

• In Lemma 9.7, the paper introduces an independent random variable U that is uniform on [0 , 1) and states P(ℓ′(λ′, U ) = k) = p(D) 

> k

. Lean represents the law of U by Lebesgue measure on [0 , 1) and expresses the same assertion as an identity of product measures. 

• The paper says in Proposition 10.3 that the entire family ( S(w)) w∈B is i.i.d. Lean expresses this by the corresponding product formula for every prefix-closed finite set of index words. The paper also identifies the realised tree with the assembly of this family, whereas the formal isometry is not required to preserve word addresses. 

• The paper derives both conclusions of Lemma 10.4 directly from the offspring law. The generic Lean lemmas instead assume the exponential moment used for the tail bound and the product factorisation used for the point-mass bound. Separate library results verify these assumptions for the shape law. 

• The statement of Lemma 10.8 records only the entropy bound, while its proof uses a contraction of each shape. Lean exposes the contraction as an address map P and records the estimate d(x, y ) ⩽ s d (P x, P y ) + 2 s − 2. At general arity it also records that the map is 2 s-marked and that the contracted tree has at most J(1 + J(s − 1)) children per vertex. Where Lemma 13.4(ii) states the result after choosing the scale from D, Lean retains the unspecialised marked constant 216 J2s2.

• The paper formulates Lemma 13.5 for the pushforward law on equivalence classes of shapes and treats the class of the one-vertex shape as one graph vertex. Lean works with shapes before taking the quotient and represents that distinguished vertex by a block of at most D2 shapes. The contribution of this block is the same error 6 e−cD 2

used in the printed proof. 

• The printed transfer result Corollary 15.3 assumes a common profile-height bound h and gives the quasi-isometry constant 72( h + 1) 2K2. The audited Lean statement replaces the two actual profile heights by the offspring bounds J, J ′ and consequently uses 216 JJ ′2K2.A separate library declaration retains the sharper dependence on the actual profile heights. 

• In the paper, Lemma 17.1 uses the exact constants Lα(β), Kα(β) and Lα. The Lean lemma permits arbitrary majorants L ⩾ Lα(β), K ⩾ Kα(β) and L0 ⩾ Lα. The four audited matching declarations choose these majorants before the Markov model, which makes their claimed uniformity part of the quantified statement. 

• The paper defines M∞(s, t ) for two independent recursively generated processes and asks for one automorphism matching all vertices. The corresponding declarations in Challenge.lean instead assert the existence of a probability space carrying two independent consistent processes and a single root-fixing automorphism of the whole infinite binary tree. The library obtains the processes from the Ionescu–Tulcea trajectory measure. 

• Theorem 1.3 is stated for a tree conditioned on infinite diameter. As for the classification, 

Challenge.lean quantifies over the surviving samples of the unconditioned law, and the binary tree B is the parent–child graph of all words over a two-letter alphabet. A THE QUASI-ISOMETRY CLASSES OF GALTON–WATSON TREES 107 

quasi-isometric embedding is the structure GraphQIEmbWith , the two metric inequalities of GraphQIWith without the density condition. 

• The proof of Lemma 23.1 bounds the binomial lower tail by Chebyshev’s inequal-ity and runs the induction at the level 3 /4. Lean bounds P(Bin( J, p ) < m ) by the union bound over the at most 2 J patterns of fewer than m surviving children, which gives 2 J (1 − p)J−⌊ J/ 2⌋ ⩽ 7/100 for p ⩾ 4/5 and J ⩾ 24, and runs the induction at the level 4 /5 ( BranchingProcess.ofReal le retainedProb ). The stated bound 3/4 follows. The exact identity (23.1) is BranchingProcess.retainedProb succ , and its fixed point for the retained root, p = θJ P(Bin( J, p ) ⩾ m), is BranchingProcess. sampleMeasure retainedInf eq ; only the induction replaces the identity by the union bound. The retained vertex of the sample is found in two ways. Along the first-child ray, as in the paper, the events An have common probability θJ p and ChainClasses. sampleMeasure spine fail gives P(τ ⩾ N ) = (1 − θJ p)N , bounded by (11 /32) N in 

ChainClasses.sampleMeasure spine fail le , with the retained vertex 1 n2 of the sam-ple in ChainClasses.ae exists spine retained . Along the descent through first sur-viving children of the skeleton under the conditioned law, ChainClasses.ae exists retained vertex uses the branching property of the surviving subtrees instead; this form does not require θ0 = 0 and is the one the proof of Theorem 1.3 uses. 

• The Galton–Watson assertion of Lemma 23.1 is stated, as the Harris decomposition is in Proposition 10.1, for the retained descendant tree of the root re-addressed by rank, BranchingProcess.retTree : under the law conditioned on the root being re-tained, BranchingProcess.retainedTreeLaw eq treeLaw identifies its law with that of a Galton–Watson tree whose offspring law BranchingProcess.Offspring.pruned is the binomial law with parameters J and p conditioned to be at least m, supported on 

{m, . . . , J }. The regular m-ary subtree rooted at a retained vertex of the sample, every vertex of which has J children in T , is BranchingProcess.exists regular embedding of retainedInf .

• The proof of Theorem 1.3 chooses a representative of a chain class from the atoms of its semigroup and treats the bushy class through the survival skeleton. Lean first trims the support bound to J = max supp θ, then uses one representative for all three supercritical classes, the mixture θ′ = 78 δJ1 + 18 θ with J1 − 1 = 24( J − 1) a multiple of a generator of Λ θ (BranchingProcess.Offspring.concentrate ), which lies in the class of θ by sameInfiniteClass concentrate . The retained vertex of the representative is found under the conditioned law as above, so the bushy case does not pass through the skeleton, and the classification transports the binary subtree in every case. The passage from the product law to the law of T uses that the representative survives with positive probability; no measurability of the embedding event is asserted. 

• The strict hierarchy Fin ≺ N0 ≺ T of §23 carries no numbered statement in the paper. Lean records it in qiEmbeddable rayGraph of not survives , not qiEmbeddable rayGraph of not survives , qiEmbeddable rayGraph of survives 

and ae not qiEmbeddable rayGraph , the last through BranchingProcess.not qiEmbeddable rayGraph , the form of Proposition 13.11 for quasi-isometric embed-dings from which the quasi-isometry statement is derived. The composition of the two embeddings for two independent laws is mutual embeddability ae .The following declarations are intentionally not formalised: Definition 2.2, Corollaries 1.5 and 3.1, Lemma 7.1, and Theorem 7.2.
